Chapter 6 Watershed Quality

In the early years of site operations, activities at Los Alamos National Laboratory (LANL or
Laboratory) released unregulated liquid wastes, which contained radionuclides, inorganic
chemicals, and organic chemicals, into nearby canyons. Efforts to reduce contaminants in these
effluents began in the 1950s. Since 1978, all effluent discharges at the site have been conducted
under regulatory permits and are treated to meet permit conditions.
Not all chemicals found in local stormwater and sediment come from site operations. Other
sources include
 • the natural composition of rocks and soils,
 • residues from trees burned in wildfires,
 • deposition of airborne radionuclides and chemicals such as polychlorinated biphenyls
           (PCBs 15), and
 • discharges or emissions from nearby towns on the Pajarito Plateau.

Both natural and manufactured sources contribute to chemical and radionuclide levels measured
in surface water and sediment across the region. We monitor chemical and radionuclide levels in
surface water and sediment for two main reasons: to assess water quality in streams within and
downstream of the site and to evaluate the potential risks to human and ecosystem health.
We compare our sampling results to
 • New Mexico water quality standards,
 • target action levels,
 • radiological dose guidelines, and
 • screening criteria for human and ecological health.

Target action levels are set in the Storm Water Individual Permit for stormwater discharges from
Solid Waste Management Units and Areas of Concern. (Refer to Chapter 2 for more information
about the permit.)

The data in this chapter come from three site programs:
 • Annual environmental surveillance sampling of stormwater runoff and sediment (N3B
           2025a, N3B 2026c, N3B 2026e)
 • Implementation of the annual Interim Facility-Wide Groundwater Monitoring Plans,
 which includes sampling of persistent surface water in streams (N3B 2024, N3B 2025b)
 • Stormwater runoff monitoring conducted under the Storm Water Individual Permit (N3B
 2026d)

15 Polychlorinated biphenyls, or PCBs, are a group of man-made organic chemicals consisting of carbon, hydrogen, and chlorine

  atoms.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 6-1

 What is success in attaining the different designated uses?
 Cold Water Aquatic Life – The water can support a coldwater aquatic life community.
 Human Health–Organism Only – The water quality protects the health of humans who eat fish or other
 aquatic wildlife.
 Limited Aquatic Life – The water can support a very limited aquatic life community.
 Livestock Watering – The water can be safely used as a drinking water source for livestock.
 Marginal Warm Water Aquatic Life – The water can support a limited warmwater aquatic life
 community.
 Primary Contact – The water quality is suitable for activities that involve prolonged human contact with
 the water, such as swimming and water skiing.
 Secondary Contact – The water quality is suitable for activities that involve limited human contact with
 the water, such as fishing and boating.
 Wildlife Habitat – The water quality is suitable to support land-based plant and animal life in the
 surrounding environment.
 Some designated-use standards for protection of aquatic life include both acute and chronic criteria.
 Acute criteria are based on toxicity to aquatic life that occurs within 96 hours, and chronic criteria are
 based on protecting aquatic life from long-term exposures.

An assessment unit is considered impaired when it fails to meet one or more of the standards
based on its designated uses. Figure 6-1 shows the locations of assessment units on and around
the site, and Table 6-1 lists the status of each designated use (supported, not supported, or not
assessed) for each assessment unit, along with the identified cause of impairment. Following
reassessment under the site-specific copper criteria (New Mexico Environment Department
2026), the New Mexico Environment Department’s 2026–2028 report removed copper as a
cause of impairment in
 • DP Canyon (100 meters downstream of grade control to 400 meters upstream of grade
 control),
 • DP Canyon (400 meters upstream of grade control to upper LANL boundary),
 • Mortandad Canyon (within LANL),
 • Pajarito Canyon (lower LANL boundary to Twomile Canyon),
 • Pueblo Canyon (Acid Canyon to headwaters), and
 • Sandia Canyon (within LANL below Sigma Canyon).

Watershed Protection Measures
We have worked with regulators and stakeholders to design and implement engineered controls
to minimize the migration of sediment and contaminants through erosion and stormwater flow.
We have installed stormwater control structures based on regulatory requirements, site
conditions, post-fire flooding risks, and best management practices. These controls are an
integral component of stormwater management at the LANL site.

Institutional Surface Water Controls
Triad National Security, LLC (Triad) manages stormwater control structures at the LANL site
that are not associated with the 2016 Compliance Order on Consent (Consent Order) or Storm
Water Individual Permit activities. The site’s infrastructure and property face perennial risks
from erosion and flooding. Stormwater controls designed to protect infrastructure are often built
alongside new facilities to maintain predevelopment runoff levels. Additional controls—located

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 6-8

in canyon bottoms or at road crossings—are designed to reduce risk to downstream facilities and
infrastructure.
Nearly 200 engineered stormwater management features are installed at the LANL site to reduce
runoff impacts and to control sediment transport. Although we constructed some of these
controls to meet regulatory requirements, no single permit or regulation governs the ongoing
maintenance and functionality of these features. We recognized the need to ensure that these
controls can perform their designed function and we have integrated their management into
existing systems, ensuring their long-term management.

Surface Water and Sediment Sampling
Surface Water Sampling Locations and Methods
We operate 38 stream gaging stations on and near the Laboratory, 36 of which have automated
samplers to collect stormwater. Additionally, we collect samples at 8 other stream channel
locations. These sampling locations are chosen to monitor surface water flow that enters and
leaves the Laboratory and former Laboratory lands, as well as at canyon confluences.
The number of gaging stations and stream channel sampling locations remains fairly constant
over time; however, not all gaging stations or channel sampling locations experience stormwater
flow in any given year. As a result, the number of locations where we collect samples can vary
widely from year to year.
We programmed the automated samplers used at gaging stations to begin collecting water 10
minutes after the peak flow during a runoff event, a method known as “Peak + 10.” The year
2025 marks the fifteenth year of employing the Peak + 10 sampling method at these stations. We
implemented this approach in response to feedback from the New Mexico Environment
Department, which noted that water samples collected before the peak of the storm flow were
highly variable and not ideal for monitoring contaminant and sediment transport. Before this
change—from 2004 to 2010—we collected samples at the peak of the runoff event. As a result,
current stormwater sampling results are not directly comparable to data collected before 2011.
We have deployed water samplers in 239 site-monitoring areas to collect stormwater runoff from
397 Solid Waste Management Units and Areas of Concern to meet monitoring requirements
under the Storm Water Individual Permit. Not all active Storm Water Individual Permit samplers
collect samples each year because rainfall on the Pajarito Plateau is often highly localized. These
samplers do not operate during months that have freezing temperatures.
Water discharged directly from springs is regulated under groundwater standards and is
discussed in Chapter 5. Water from springs that has infiltrated into canyon bottoms and has
resurfaced as base flow is regulated under surface water standards. We collected grab samples of
base flow at locations identified in the “Interim Facility-Wide Groundwater Monitoring Plan for
the 2025 Monitoring Year, October 2024–September 2025” and the “Interim Facility-Wide
Groundwater Monitoring Plan for the 2026 Monitoring Year, October 2025–September 2026”
(N3B 2024, N3B 2025b).
Figure 6-2 shows locations where we collected samples in 2025 for stormwater at stream gaging
stations and for base flow. Figure 6-3 shows Storm Water Individual Permit site-monitoring

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 6-9

areas where we collected compliance samples in 2025. We collected 36 compliance samples
from 25 Storm Water Individual Permit site-monitoring areas in 2025.

Figure 6-3. Individual Permit (IP) site monitoring areas where automated samplers collected stormwater

Sediment Sampling Locations and Methods
For the LANL site, we define sediment as any soil that is either suspended in water or deposited
by surface water flow. Figure 6-4 shows locations sampled for sediment in 2025. We collected
samples at depths that ranged from 0 to 1 inch, depending on the thickness of the uppermost
sediment layer. We took samples from stream channels and floodplains where new sediment had
deposited during 2025. For streams with flowing water, we collected sediment samples near the
edge of the main channel, adjacent to but not in the water. In 2025, stormwater runoff occurred
in every canyon, allowing for sediment sampling across all major watersheds.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 6-11

Figure 6-4. Locations sampled in 2025 for sediment as part of the annual environmental surveillance
 program.

Note: A or AN = Ancho Canyon; ABV = above; ACS = Acid Canyon; BKG = background; BLW = below; CdB = Cañada del
 Buey; LA = Los Alamos Canyon; M or Mort = Mortandad Canyon; MDA = Material disposal area; P = Pueblo Canyon;
 PA = Pajarito Canyon; RG = Rio Grande; S = Sandia Canyon; WA = Water Canyon; @ = at

Surface Water Screening Levels
We follow a protocol published by the New Mexico Environment Department to assess if surface
waters meet assigned state standards (New Mexico Environment Department 2025). We use
water hardness values from concurrent samples to calculate hardness-dependent aquatic life
criteria for metals (U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 2006a, Water Quality Control
Commission 2022). On January 14, 2025, the New Mexico Water Quality Control Commission

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 6-12

approved a site-specific water-quality criterion for copper on the Pajarito Plateau and published
the criterion in the New Mexico Administrative Code on May 22, 2025.
U.S. Department of Energy (DOE) Order 458.1 Chg 4, Radiation Protection of the Public and the
Environment, sets limits on the total dose of radioactivity that Laboratory operations may
release. These limits apply to the public, plants, and animals. Therefore, the radiological
assessment of surface water considers potential exposure to both aquatic organisms and land-
dwelling animals (collectively referred to as “biota”).
We use the DOE biota concentration guides (DOE 2019), along with site-specific modifications
by McNaughton et al. (2013), as screening levels to evaluate radioactivity in surface water. We
use biota concentration guides for aquatic, riparian, or terrestrial animals depending on how
often surface water is present at a given location. For perennial and intermittent reaches, we
apply aquatic, riparian, and terrestrial animal biota concentration guides. For ephemeral reaches,
we use terrestrial animal biota concentration guides. Refer to Chapter 7 for biota dose results. In
Chapter 8, we evaluate human health risks associated with stormwater exposure.
We also use the New Mexico water quality standards to evaluate surface water results for gross
alpha radioactivity and radium isotopes; however, the gross alpha standard does not apply to
source, special nuclear, or byproduct material regulated by the DOE under the Atomic Energy
Act of 1954. The gross alpha radioactivity data presented in this chapter have not been adjusted
to exclude these sources of radioactivity.
We evaluate surface water results from Storm Water Individual Permit site-monitoring areas
using target action levels provided in the Permit. The 2025 Annual Sampling Implementation
Plan, NPDES Permit No. NM0030759 (N3B 2026b), provides additional details on site-
monitoring area results.

Sediment Screening Levels
We evaluate radioactivity in sediment using risk-based screening action levels (LANL 2015) and
the DOE biota concentration guides (DOE 2019) with site-specific modifications by
McNaughton et al. (2013). We use biota concentration guides for riparian and terrestrial animals
in these evaluations. Chapter 7 provides biota dose results. In Chapter 8, we evaluate human
health risks associated with sediment exposure.
We evaluate chemical levels using the New Mexico Environment Department’s risk-based soil
screening levels (New Mexico Environment Department 2022) for chemicals that could cause
cancer or harmful health effects. If a chemical poses both cancer and noncancer risks, we provide
separate screening levels for each risk type. When no New Mexico Environment Department
risk-based soil screening levels are available for a chemical, we use the U.S. Environmental
Protection Agency’s regional screening level (U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 2024).
The soil screening levels for inorganic and organic chemicals and the screening action levels for
radionuclides reflect levels that are considered safe for different exposure scenarios: what is safe
for humans in industrial settings, what is safe for construction workers, and what is safe for
residential exposure. If concentrations are below both the screening action levels and the soil
screening levels, adverse human health effects are highly unlikely.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 6-13

These screening levels provide a high level of confidence in determining a low probability of
risk to human health; however, they are not intended to provide definitive risk estimates and
might not reflect current land use (U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 2001). For example,
we compare samples from onsite locations to all screening levels—including the residential
exposure scenario levels—even though no residences exist on site.

Results

2025 Precipitation and Surface Water Runoff
Total surface water that left the LANL site—as measured at downstream gaging stations—was
117 acre-feet during October 1, 2024, through September 30, 2025 (the 2025 water year), and 40
acre-feet during June 1, 2025, through October 31, 2025 (the monsoonal and tropical storm
period for 2025).
Figure 6-5 shows the amount of precipitation across the LANL site and stormwater runoff at the
site’s most downstream gaging stations during the monsoonal and tropical storm period (June 1
through October 31) from 2012 to 2025.

Figure 6-5. Total June–October precipitation from 2012 to 2025 averaged across the Laboratory’s
 meteorological tower network (Technical Area 06, Technical Area 49, Technical Area 53,
 Technical Area 54, and northern community), and estimated June–October stormwater
 runoff volume leaving the Laboratory, measured at the most downstream gaging stations,
              from 2012 to 2025.

In 2025, precipitation during the monsoon and tropical storm periods was 10.4 inches. We
measured most of the stormwater runoff that reached the furthest downstream (eastern) gaging
stations during the monsoonal and tropical storm periods. Table 6-2 presents the results.

Snowmelt runoff reached the furthest downstream (eastern) gaging stations in Los Alamos,
Chaquehui, Pajarito, and Mortandad canyon watersheds during the 2025 water year (October 1,
2024, through September 30, 2025). Total snowmelt runoff at these stations is estimated at 64
acre-feet, with 40 acre-feet occurring in Chaquehui Canyon.
During the 2025 water year, 596 acre-feet of effluent was released from the Los Alamos Canyon
Wastewater Treatment Facility in Pueblo Canyon. During winter and spring months, it is
common for frozen soils and/or senescent vegetation to permit limited surface flow to reach
gaging station E060.1 in lower Pueblo Canyon. Twenty-eight acre-feet, inclusive of effluent,
snowmelt, and stormwater, was measured at the E060.1 gaging station during the 2025 water
year.

Constituents in Stormwater Samples
In 2025, we collected stormwater from 26 locations and base flow samples from 8 locations. For
inorganic chemicals, 5 locations had no exceedances of their applicable New Mexico water
quality standard, 5 locations had one exceedance, 8 locations had two exceedances, and 15
locations had more than two exceedances. For organic chemicals and radionuclides, 18 locations
had no exceedances, 5 locations had one exceedance, 5 locations had two exceedances, and 6
locations had more than 2 exceedances. Surface water monitoring data for 2025 and previous
years are available on the IntellusNM website (https://intellusnm.com). Table 6-3 summarizes
inorganic chemical results for 2025 stormwater and base flow samples, and Table 6-4 provides a
summary of organic chemical and radionuclide results for 2025 stormwater and base flow
samples. Table 6-5 summarizes surface water exceedances in 2025, showing the percentage of
all locations analyzed for each chemical or radioactive constituent with an exceedance.
The tables do not include compliance sampling results for the Storm Water Individual Permit;
however, we discuss these results in the text and display results in the figures within section
Discussion and Trends. Tables that contain the Storm Water Individual Permit sampling results
for 2025 are available in the 2025 Update to the Site Discharge Pollution Prevention Plan (N3B
2026d).

Constituents in Sediment Samples
In 2025, exceedances of screening levels for sediment samples were minimal. Of the 76 regular
sediment samples collected, only 12 had exceedances of at least one screening level. Eight
chemicals accounted for these exceedances, with six being a PFAS compound. No chemical
level in a sediment sample exceeded its residential cancer, industrial cancer, or construction
work cancer soil screening level. All radionuclide levels in 2025 sediment were below screening
action levels and DOE biota concentration guides. Table 6-6 highlights chemical results from
2025 sediment samples where at least one chemical exceeded screening levels.

Discussion and Trends
The following sections discuss the 2025 stormwater, base flow, and sediment results.
Constituents are grouped based on their likely sources (background or Laboratory operations).
Most 2025 stormwater and base flow results fell within the concentration ranges observed from
2011 to 2024. Notable exceptions include elevated iron concentrations in parts of the Mortandad
Canyon and Pajarito Canyon watersheds.
Figure 6-6 through Figure 6-22 present analytical results for specific constituents by location. In
each figure, the top panel displays sample locations (stream gaging stations, sediment detention
basins, base flow locations, and Storm Water Individual Permit site monitoring areas) marked by
colored circles. The color of each circle reflects the maximum constituent concentration at that
location from 2011 to 2025 relative to other locations in the same watershed. Blue represents the
lowest 10 percent of maximum concentrations and orange represents the highest 10 percent. The
range of concentration values represented by each color appears at the top of each figure.
The bottom panel of each figure provides time-series graphs that show detected stormwater and
base flow results for the constituent from 2011 through 2025. Assorted colors distinguish Storm
Water Individual Permit samples and stream gaging station samples.

Constituents Related to Natural and Manufactured Background Sources
The following sections discuss chemicals that are primarily naturally occurring or that originate
from sources other than site operations.

Aluminum
Aluminum is commonly found in stormwater across the Pajarito Plateau, often exceeding New
Mexico water quality standards; however, most or all surface water aluminum is naturally
occurring because it is a natural component of local soils and the Bandelier Tuff (Reneau et al.
2010, Ryan et al. 2019). Laboratory operations do not produce aluminum in significant
quantities. The New Mexico Environment Department Surface Water Quality Bureau has
acknowledged that “natural conditions may contribute to high aluminum concentrations in the
Jemez Mountains” (New Mexico Environment Department 2024).
In 2025, total aluminum concentrations in stormwater and base flow samples exceeded an acute
aquatic life criterion at 23 locations (72 percent of locations where the standard applied) and a
chronic aquatic life criterion at 13 locations (76 percent of locations). The single Storm Water
Individual Permit compliance sample analyzed for aluminum exceeded the permit’s target action
level. Of the 44 assessment units on Laboratory or former Laboratory lands, 16 are listed as
impaired for aluminum (Table 6-1).
In 2025, no sediment samples exceeded soil screening levels for aluminum.

Arsenic
Arsenic originates from both natural and human-made sources. It occurs naturally in local
volcanic rocks. Manufactured sources include coal-fired power plants.
In 2025, arsenic levels in filtered stormwater and base flow samples did not exceed applicable
New Mexico surface water quality standards. We did not analyze Storm Water Individual Permit

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 6-22

compliance samples for arsenic. None of the 44 assessment units on Laboratory or former
Laboratory lands are listed as impaired for arsenic (Table 6-1).
In 2025, no sediment samples exceeded soil screening levels for arsenic.

Copper
Copper is naturally occurring but can also come from sources such as explosives firing sites,
forest fires, and urban infrastructure. In developed areas, copper is often associated with brake
pad wear and building materials such as plumbing and electrical components (TDC
Environmental 2004, Göbel et al. 2007). Historically, we have observed elevated copper
concentrations in stormwater across all watersheds at the Laboratory, including at gaging stations
located along the upstream boundary.
In 2025, copper concentrations in filtered stormwater and base flow samples did not exceed
applicable New Mexico surface water quality standards. Of the 13 Storm Water Individual
Permit compliance samples analyzed for copper, 8 exceeded the permit’s target action level. Of
the 44 assessment units on Laboratory or former Laboratory lands, 10 are listed as impaired for
copper (Table 6-1).
Figure 6-6 through Figure 6-9 show copper concentrations in filtered stormwater and base flow
across the Ancho Canyon and Chaquehui Canyon watersheds, the Los Alamos Canyon
watershed (including Pueblo Canyon), the Pajarito Canyon watershed, and the Mortandad
Canyon and Sandia Canyon watersheds, respectively. Copper concentrations measured in 2025
were consistent with those observed in previous years.
In 2025, no sediment samples exceeded soil screening levels for copper.

Iron
Iron is a naturally occurring element and is also associated with explosives firing sites. A surface
water quality standard for total iron was established in 2022. In 2025, iron concentrations in
stormwater and base flow samples exceeded a chronic aquatic life criterion at 14 locations (82
percent of locations). The Storm Water Individual Permit does not include a target action level
for iron; we screen iron data to applicable water quality standards, but exceedances do not trigger
corrective action. None of the 44 assessment units on Laboratory or former Laboratory lands are
listed as impaired for iron (Table 6-1).
Figure 6-10 through Figure 6-12 show iron concentrations in stormwater and base flow for the
Los Alamos Canyon watershed (including Pueblo Canyon), the Mortandad Canyon and Sandia
Canyon watersheds, and the Pajarito Canyon watershed, respectively.
In 2025, no sediment samples exceeded soil screening levels for iron.

Lead
Lead is associated with explosives firing sites as well as buildings and parking lots in developed
areas (Göbel et al. 2007). Common sources of lead in urban settings include lead-based paints,
building sidings, and emission from vehicle operations (Davis and Burns 1999).
In 2025, lead concentrations in filtered stormwater and base flow exceeded a chronic aquatic life
criterion at 7 locations (41 percent of locations). The single Storm Water Individual Permit
compliance sample analyzed for lead did not exceed the permit’s target action level. None of the
44 assessment units on Laboratory or former Laboratory lands are listed as impaired for lead
(Table 6-1).
Figure 6-13 presents lead concentrations in filtered stormwater and base flow for the Mortandad
Canyon and Sandia Canyon watersheds. Lead concentrations measured in 2025 were consistent
with those observed in previous years.
In 2025, no sediment samples exceeded soil screening levels for lead.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 6-32

 panels: detected dissolved lead concentrations from IP, gaging station, and base flow
 samples from 2011 to 2025. Note: µg/L = microgram per liter.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 6-33

Manganese
Manganese is naturally occurring on the Pajarito Plateau. Laboratory operations have not
historically generated manganese in significant quantities. Following the Cerro Grande Fire,
dissolved manganese concentrations temporarily increased but declined in subsequent years
(Gallaher and Koch 2004, 2005).
In 2025, manganese concentrations in stormwater and base flow samples did not exceed
applicable water quality standards. The Storm Water Individual Permit does not establish a target
action level for manganese. None of the 44 assessment units on Laboratory or former Laboratory
lands are listed as impaired for manganese (Table 6-1).
In 2025, manganese concentrations exceeded the construction worker noncancer soil screening
level in three sediment samples.

Selenium
Selenium is naturally occurring on the Pajarito Plateau and Laboratory operations have not
contributed significant quantities. Like manganese, selenium concentrations were elevated
following the Cerro Grande Fire but decreased in subsequent years (Gallaher and Koch 2004,
2005).
In 2025, total selenium concentrations in stormwater and base flow exceeded the acute aquatic
life criterion at 6 locations (19 percent of locations), a chronic aquatic life criterion at 3 locations
(18 percent of locations), and the wildlife habitat standard at 9 locations (28 percent of
locations). We did not analyze Storm Water Individual Permit compliance samples for selenium.
Of the 44 assessment units on Laboratory or former Laboratory lands, 3 are listed as impaired for
selenium (Table 6-1).
In 2025, no sediment samples exceeded soil screening levels for selenium.

Zinc
Zinc is naturally occurring and is also commonly associated with developed areas. Sources of
zinc in urban environments include automobile tires, galvanized materials, motor oil, and
hydraulic fluid (Rose et al. 2001, Councell et al. 2004, Washington State Department of Ecology
2006).
In 2025, filtered zinc concentrations in stormwater and base flow samples exceeded the acute
aquatic life criterion at 4 locations (13 percent of locations) and the chronic aquatic life criterion
at 4 locations (24 percent of locations). Two of the five Storm Water Individual Permit
compliance samples analyzed for zinc exceeded the target action level. None of the 44
assessment units on Laboratory or former Laboratory lands are listed as impaired for zinc (Table
6-1).
Figure 6-14 shows zinc concentrations in filtered stormwater and base flow for the Mortandad
Canyon and Sandia Canyon watersheds. Zinc concentrations measured in 2025 were consistent
with those observed in previous years.
In 2025, no sediment samples exceeded soil screening levels for zinc.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 6-34

 panels: detected dissolved zinc concentrations from IP, gaging station, and base flow
 samples from 2011 to 2025. Note: µg/L = microgram per liter.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 6-35

Gross Alpha
Gross alpha activity measures the combined radioactivity from alpha-emitting isotopes,
including naturally occurring isotopes of radium, thorium, and uranium and their decay products.
We observed elevated gross alpha activities in stormwater samples during 2011 through 2013,
particularly in samples affected by ash and sediment from the 2011 Las Conchas Fire and the
large flood event in September 2013.
In 2025, stormwater samples from 14 locations (52 percent of locations) exceeded the livestock
watering standard for gross alpha activity. The Storm Water Individual Permit does not include a
target action level for gross alpha. We screen gross alpha data to applicable water quality
standards, but exceedances do not trigger corrective action. Of the 44 assessment units on
Laboratory or former Laboratory lands, 29 are listed as impaired for gross alpha radioactivity
(Table 6-1).
Figure 6-15 through Figure 6-18 show gross alpha concentrations in stormwater and base flow
across the Ancho Canyon and Chaquehui Canyon watersheds, the Los Alamos Canyon
watershed (including Pueblo Canyon), the Pajarito Canyon watersheds, and the Mortandad
Canyon and Sandia Canyon watersheds, respectively. Results from 2025 are consistent with
previous findings, which confirm that most gross alpha radioactivity in stormwater on the
Pajarito Plateau originates from the natural decay of isotopes in sediment and soil, with relatively
small contributions from Laboratory operations (Gallaher 2007).
We do not analyze sediment samples for gross alpha activity; instead, sediment analysis targets
specific radionuclides of concern at designated locations.

Radium-226 and Radium-228
Radium is a naturally occurring radioactive element that forms from the decay of uranium and
thorium. In the past, radium was used in industrial and commercial applications and is still used
for a few such purposes today (U.S. Nuclear Regulatory Commission 2025). It is found at trace
levels in nearly all rock, soil, water, plants, and animals. Some regions have higher
concentrations due to local geology; for example, elevated radium levels can be associated with
uranium deposits (U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 2022).
In 2025, radium-226 and radium-228 concentrations in stormwater and base flow samples did
not exceed applicable water quality standards. One Storm Water Individual Permit compliance
sample was analyzed for radium-226 and radium-228 and did not exceed the target action level.
Of the 44 assessment units on Laboratory or former Laboratory lands, 1 is listed as impaired for
radium (Table 6-1).
Previous analytical results confirm that most of the radium-226 and radium-228 found in
stormwater on the Pajarito Plateau originates from the natural decay of isotopes present in local
sediment and soil (Gallaher 2007).

Constituents Related to Los Alamos National Laboratory Operations
In stormwater, base flow, and sediment, we detected several constituents known to have been
released during past site operations. We have described the nature and extent of the constituents
in sediment in detail in the canyons’ investigation reports (LANL 2004, 2005, 2006, 2009a,
2009b, 2009c, 2009d, 2011a, 2011b, 2011c).
The following sections describe site-related constituents in 2025 stormwater, base flow, and
sediment samples.

Cadmium
Cadmium is associated with the combustion of fossil fuels, industrial processes such as the
refinement of nickel-cadmium batteries, metal plating, pigments, and plastics, as well as
activities such as sewage sludge disposal and the application of phosphate fertilizers (Agency for
Toxic Substances and Disease Registry 2012).
In 2025, dissolved cadmium in stormwater and base flow exceeded the acute aquatic life
criterion at 1 location (3 percent of locations) and chronic aquatic life criterion at 2 locations (12
percent of locations). We did not analyze Storm Water Individual Permit compliance samples for
cadmium. None of the 44 assessment units on Laboratory or former Laboratory lands are listed
as impaired for cadmium (Table 6-1).
In 2025, no sediment results exceeded soil screening levels for cadmium.

Chromium
Chromium is associated with the historical use of potassium dichromate as a corrosion inhibitor
in the cooling system at the Technical Area 03 power plant (LANL 1973). This compound was
discharged through Outfall 001 during 1956 to 1972.
In 2025, filtered stormwater and base flow results did not exceed surface water quality standards
for either total chromium or hexavalent chromium. We did not analyze Storm Water Individual

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 6-41

Permit compliance samples for chromium or hexavalent chromium. None of the 44 assessment
units for surface waters on Laboratory or former Laboratory lands are listed as impaired for
chromium (Table 6-1).
In 2025, no sediment results exceeded soil screening levels for chromium.

Dioxins and Furans
Dioxins and furans are associated with the incineration of medical, industrial, municipal, and
private wastes; municipal wastewater treatment sludge; coal-fired boilers; and diesel fuel
emissions (U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 2006b). Forest fires are also a significant
natural source of dioxins (Gullett and Touati 2003). Toxic equivalents are used to report the
toxicity-weighted mass of dioxin and furan mixtures, providing a more meaningful measure of
toxicity than the mass of individual compounds (U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 2010).
Surface water quality standards apply to total dioxin toxic equivalents but not to individual
dioxins or furans.
In 2025, total dioxin concentrations in stormwater or base flow samples exceeded the human
health–organism only standard at the one location where it applied. The single Storm Water
Individual Permit compliance sample analyzed for 2,3,7,8-tetrachlorodibenzodioxin did not
exceed the target action level. None of the 44 assessment units on Laboratory or former
Laboratory lands are listed as impaired for dioxins or furans (Table 6-1).
In 2025, no sediment samples exceeded soil screening levels for dioxins or furans.

Mercury
Mercury originates from both natural sources—such as forest fires—and from human
activities—including coal combustion and mining. Although regional coal-fired power plants
contributed to regional mercury contamination, the Laboratory also historically operated
coal-fired power plants that could have been sources.
In 2025, mercury concentrations in stormwater and base flow did not exceed surface water
quality standards. Neither of the two Storm Water Individual Permit compliance samples
analyzed for mercury exceeded the target action level. Of the 44 assessment units on Laboratory
or former Laboratory lands, 6 are listed as impaired for mercury (Table 6-1).
In 2025, no sediment samples exceeded soil screening levels for mercury.

Polychlorinated Biphenyls
PCBs are stable, persistent organic compounds that break down slowly in the environment. They
were commonly used as plastic and paint stabilizers and coolants in electrical appliances before
being banned in the United States in 1979. Many legacy construction materials contain PCBs,
including caulking, paints, window putty, and electrical components (Durell and Lizotte 1998,
Kakareka and Kukharchyk 2006). As these materials weather and deteriorate, PCBs accumulate
in the environment and are redistributed through surface runoff and atmospheric deposition
(Chevreuil et al. 1996, Duinker and Bouchertall 1989, Grainer et al. 1990, LANL 2012). The
Laboratory historically used materials that contained PCBs, such as transformer fluids, solvents,
paints; operations at a former asphalt batch plant in Sandia Canyon also involved PCBs. Some
buildings that contain legacy PCB materials still exist on the LANL site.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 6-42

In 2025, total Aroclor concentrations in stormwater and base flow exceeded the human health–
organism only standard at 2 locations (8 percent of locations), a chronic aquatic life criterion at 1
location (6 percent of locations), and the wildlife habitat standard at 2 locations (8 percent of
locations). Of the 12 Storm Water Individual Permit compliance samples analyzed for total PCB
concentrations, 8 exceeded the target action level. Of the 44 assessment units on Laboratory or
former Laboratory lands, 33 are listed as impaired for PCBs (Table 6-1).
Figure 6-19 and Figure 6-20 present total PCB congener and total PCB Aroclor concentrations in
unfiltered stormwater and base flow for Los Alamos Canyon watershed, including Pueblo
Canyon and the Mortandad Canyon and Sandia Canyon watersheds, respectively.
In 2025, two sediment samples collected from Los Alamos Canyon exceeded the residential
noncancer soil screening level for Aroclor-1254.

Polycyclic Aromatic Hydrocarbons
Polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons are a group of chemicals commonly found in petroleum
products, including asphalt. A former asphalt batch plant near Sandia Canyon discharged
wastewater that contained polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons into the canyon.
In 2025, polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons concentrations in stormwater and base flow exceeded
the human health–organism only standard at 1 location (13 percent of locations). Exceedances
occurred for 6 of the 19 polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons with water quality standards:
benzo(a)anthracene, benzo(a)pyrene, benzo(b)fluoranthene, benzo(k)fluoranthene,
dibenz(a,h)anthracene, and indeno(1,2,3-cd)pyrene. None of the 10 Storm Water Individual
Permit compliance samples analyzed for polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons exceeded target
action levels. None of the 44 assessment units on Laboratory or former Laboratory lands are
listed as impaired for polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (Table 6-1). Figure 6-21 shows total
indeno(1,2,3-cd)pyrene concentrations in unfiltered stormwater and base flow for the Sandia
Canyon watershed.
In 2025, no sediment samples exceeded screening levels for polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons.

Several radionuclides are associated with site operations. In 2025, no stormwater or sediment
samples exceeded applicable water quality standards or screening levels for radionuclides.

Silver
Silver associated with site operations has been found in Pajarito Canyon and Cañon de Valle
(LANL 2009a, LANL 2011c).
In 2025, dissolved silver in filtered stormwater and base flow samples exceeded the acute aquatic
life standard at 2 locations (6 percent of locations). The single Storm Water Individual Permit
compliance sample analyzed for silver did not exceed the target action level. Of the 44
assessment units on Laboratory or former Laboratory lands, 2 are listed as impaired for silver
(Table 6-1).
In 2025, no sediment results exceeded soil screening levels for silver.

Thallium
Thallium emissions have been associated with cement factories and coal-fired power plants.
Although regional coal-fired power plants could have contributed to thallium contamination in
the region, the Laboratory historically operated coal-fired power plants that could have been
sources.
In 2025, no filtered stormwater or base flow samples exceeded surface water quality standards
for thallium. We did not analyze Storm Water Individual Permit compliance samples for
thallium. None of the 44 assessment units on Laboratory or former Laboratory lands are listed as
impaired for thallium (Table 6-1).
In 2025, no sediment results exceeded soil screening levels for thallium.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 6-50

Summary – PFAS Monitoring Results
No surface water quality standards have been established for PFAS, although we conduct PFAS
analyses on select samples for informational purposes. In 2025, PFAS were detected in
stormwater or base flow samples at 15 locations. Fifteen Storm Water Individual Permit
locations were selected for PFAS analysis; however, none of the locations received sufficient
flow in 2025 to be sampled. One location had already completed its required PFAS analysis in
previous years. Additional information can be found in the “2025 Annual Data Report for
Per- and Polyfluoroalkyl Substances in Storm Water” (N3B 2026a).
The New Mexico Environment Department and the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency have
established screening levels for some PFAS compounds in soils. In 2025, PFAS concentrations
in sediment did not exceed the New Mexico Environment Department’s screening levels. We
compared results for PFAS compounds that have no New Mexico Environment Department soil
screening levels with U.S. Environmental Protection Agency’s regional screening levels. This
resulted in seven exceedances for resident soil levels and three exceedances for industrial soil
levels. We will continue to monitor PFAS in sediment.
Figure 6-26 through Figure 6-28 show 2025 concentrations in stormwater and base flow for the
PFAS compounds perfluorohexane-sulfonic acid (PFHxS), perfluorooctanesulfonic acid (PFOS),
and perfluorooctanoic acid (PFOA) in the DP Canyon and Los Alamos Canyon watersheds, the
Pueblo Canyon subwatershed, and the Sandia Canyon watershed.

Figure 6-26. Perfluorohexanesulfonic acid (PFHxS), perfluorooctanesulfonic acid (PFOS), and
 perfluorooctanoic acid (PFOA) concentrations in Los Alamos subwatersheds stormwater and
 baseflow samples in 2025.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 6-51

Figure 6-27. Perfluorohexanesulfonic acid (PFHxS), perfluorooctanesulfonic acid (PFOS), and
 perfluorooctanoic acid (PFOA) concentrations in Pueblo subwatersheds stormwater and
 baseflow samples in 2025.

Figure 6-28. Perfluorohexanesulfonic acid (PFHxS), perfluorooctanesulfonic acid (PFOS), and
 perfluorooctanoic acid (PFOA) concentrations in Sandia watershed stormwater and baseflow
 samples in 2025.

We perform sampling of storm flow, base flow, and sediment and measure stream flow
according to written quality assurance and quality control procedures and protocols. Current

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 6-52

versions of all procedures and guides can be found at https://eprr.em-la.doe.gov/. These
procedures ensure that the collection, processing, and chemical analysis of samples and the
validation and verification of analytical data are consistent from year to year.
Analytical results meet the Newport News Nuclear BWXT-Los Alamos, LLC (N3B) minimum
data quality objectives as outlined in N3B-PLN-SDM-1000, “Sample and Data Management
Plan.” This plan sets the validation frequency criteria at 100 percent Level 1 examination and
Level 2 verification of data and at 10 percent minimum Level 3 validation of data.
 • A Level 1 examination assesses the completeness of the data as delivered from the
 analytical laboratory, identifies any reporting errors, and checks the usability of the data
 based on the analytical laboratory’s evaluation of the data.
 • A Level 2 verification evaluates the data to determine the extent to which the laboratory
 met the analytical method and the contract-specific quality control and reporting
 requirements.
 • A Level 3 validation includes Levels 1 and 2 criteria and determines the effect of
 potential anomalies encountered during analysis and possible effects on data quality and
 usability. A Level 3 validation is performed manually with method-specific data
 validation procedures.
N3B personnel validate laboratory analytical data as outlined in N3B-PLN-SDM-1000;
N3B-AP-SDM-3000, “General Guidelines for Data Validation”; N3B-AP-SDM-3014,
“Examination and Verification of Analytical Laboratory Data”; and additional method-specific
analytical data validation procedures. All associated validation procedures have been developed,
where applicable, from the Department of Defense and Department of Energy Quality Systems
Manual for Environmental Laboratories, Environmental Protection Agency National Functional
Guidelines, Guidance on Environmental Data Verification and Data Validation (EPA QA/G-8),
Multi-Agency Radiological Laboratory Analytical Protocols Manual, and American National
Standard Verification and Validation of Radiological Data for Use in Waste Management and
Environmental Remediation (ANSI/ANS 41.5). If a sample is mishandled or the analytical data
are rejected, the affected sampled analyses will be replanned.

The monitoring data continue to support our current conceptual model: that stormwater runoff in
Laboratory canyons generally deposits sediment with concentrations of site-related substances
that are equal to or lower than the concentrations observed in previous years. Through our
ongoing surveillance program, we monitor the movement and concentration of contaminants in
sediment over time and take action to mitigate or reduce sediment transport where needed.
The 2025 stormwater, base flow, and sediment data confirm the conceptual model. The results
also support previous risk assessments presented in the canyons’ investigation reports (LANL
2004, 2005, 2006, 2009a, 2009b, 2009c, 2009d, 2011a, 2011b, 2011c), which represent the upper
bound of potential human and ecological health risks in these watersheds.
Concentrations of chemicals in storm flow and base flow samples remained within or below
historical ranges except for elevated iron levels in Mortandad Canyon and Pajarito Canyon
watersheds. In Sandia Canyon, total PCB concentrations have shown an increasing trend in
recent years; we will continue to monitor these concentrations.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 6-53

In 2025, sediment exceedances were limited and included manganese, Aroclor-1254, and several
PFAS chemicals. We evaluate sediment results over multiple years and compare results with
nearby surface water data to assess long-term trends and to identify spatial patterns.
Based on the human health risk assessments in the canyons’ investigation reports, along with the
biota dose assessment (Chapter 7) and the human health risk assessment (Chapter 8) in this
report, the cumulative total concentrations of chemicals and radionuclides in stormwater, base
flow, and sediment are below levels that would impact human or biota health.
Our ongoing maintenance and construction of watershed-scale engineered controls continue to
effectively minimize the downstream migration of contaminated sediment to the Rio Grande.

Chapter 7 Ecosystem Health

An ecosystem includes living organisms such as plants, animals, and bacteria; nonliving
elements such as soil, air, and water; and the interactions among these components (Smith and
Smith 2012). How an ecosystem functions is affected by disturbances, including wildfire,
flooding, drought, invasive species, chemical spills, construction projects, and vegetation
removal, among others (Rapport 1998).
To evaluate and support the health of our local ecosystems, we monitor and, in some cases,
manage
 • levels of radionuclide and chemical constituents in soil, sediment, plants, and animals;
 • federally listed threatened or endangered species;
 • populations of migratory bird species and other species of concern;
 • state-listed threatened or endangered species or species of greatest conservation need;
 • forest conditions;
 • locations of invasive plant species; and
 • new erosion areas.

Our objectives are to
 • determine if operations at the Los Alamos National Laboratory (LANL or Laboratory)
 site affect plant or animal populations (collectively called “biota”);
 • meet federal and state regulatory requirements;
 • minimize future risk to ecological resources;
 • reduce the potential for harm from wildland fire;
 • increase forest and habitat resilience to disturbances; and
 • implement the Invasive Plant Species Management Plan (LANL 2024a) and Pollinator
 Protection Plan (LANL 2024b).
We rotate some types of institutional monitoring we perform on a three-year cycle (Figure 7-1).
In 2025, we collected foodstuffs—such as wild foods, crops, milk, tea, eggs, and honey—as part
of our soil, foodstuffs, and biota monitoring program. Foodstuffs results are reported Chapter 8.

Constituent Levels in Soil and Sediment
Chemicals that are released into the air or that are attached to particles transported by wind and
water are eventually deposited onto soil or sediment. Monitoring soil over time directly measures
long-term trends in levels of radionuclides and other chemicals around nuclear facilities (DOE
2015).

Constituent Levels in Plant and Animal Tissues
We also directly measure levels of constituents in animal and
 Biota dose screening levels
plant tissues. These measurements are compared with the Set at 10 percent of the U.S.
lowest concentration measured in a plant or in an animal’s Department of Energy (DOE)
tissues that is associated with an adverse effect (called the limit for radiation doses to biota
lowest observable adverse effect level) when those levels are (DOE 2019, McNaughton 2021).

Estimated Doses to Plants and Animals
We estimate biota radiation dose and chemical risk using dose and risk models developed or
approved by the U.S. Department of Energy (DOE) or the State of New Mexico. The estimated
dose from radiation to biota is calculated using RESRAD-BIOTA software (version 1.8;
https://resrad.evs.anl.gov/codes/resrad-biota/), which is a DOE methodology for estimating
radiation doses to aquatic and terrestrial plants and animals. This calculated dose is compared
with DOE limits: 1 rad per day for terrestrial plants and aquatic animals and 0.1 rad per day for
terrestrial animals (DOE 2019).

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-4

Comparisons among Sites and over Time
We perform statistical tests to evaluate differences in constituents among sites and to examine
trends in constituent levels over time. As required by the DOE, soil background locations are at a
similar elevation to the Laboratory site (most between 7,000 and 8,000 feet above sea level),
more than 9.3 miles away from the Laboratory boundaries, and beyond the range of potential
influence from normal Laboratory operations (DOE 2015). Samples collected within the past 10
years or so are used to study trends over time. Samples from this time frame are directly
comparable because they were analyzed with similar analytical methods and instruments and
have similar detection limits.
We test a null hypothesis of no effect for each set of data. For each test, we select a probability
level, or p-value, of the null hypothesis being correct, and then we accept or reject the null
hypothesis. A p-value of less than 5 percent (p < 0.05) is used as our threshold to reject the null
hypothesis of no difference between locations or no trend over time. If the p-value is greater than
5 percent (p > 0.05), we accept the null hypothesis of no difference or no trend. Statistical
analyses are not conducted on datasets in which 80 percent or more of the results for a specific
chemical or radionuclide are not detected (Helsel 2012).

 What does it mean if a chemical is not detected?
 When an analytical laboratory tests a sample for the presence of a chemical, the test results come
 back either detected or not detected. Generally, an analytical laboratory test cannot determine if a
 very small amount of the chemical exists in a sample. The level of the chemical in a sample that the
 laboratory can detect with its test is called the detection limit. If the chemical is detected, the
 analytical laboratory reports the amount of the chemical in the sample. If the chemical is not detected,
 it means the amount of the chemical in the sample is somewhere between zero and the detection
 limit. When a laboratory reports a nondetected result, it can report a value of 0, the detection limit, or
 some fraction of the detection limit. How the result is reported can affect any statistical test that
 includes the nondetected result.

Results of Facility-Specific Monitoring for Radionuclides and Chemicals

Area G at Technical Area 54
Area G was established in 1957 and is the site’s primary transuranic waste and low-level
radioactive solid waste burial and storage area (DOE 1979, Martinez 2006; Figure 7-4). Tritium,
plutonium, americium, and uranium are the main radionuclides in waste materials at Area G
(Mayfield and Hansen 1983). We have conducted soil and vegetation testing at Area G since
1980 to monitor if radionuclides are migrating beyond the waste burial area (LANL 1981,
Mayfield and Hansen 1983).
We collect surface soil and vegetation at Area G each year for testing. We collected surface soil
grab samples (0 to 6 inches deep) and composite tree samples—primarily of one-seed juniper
(Juniperus monosperma) and Gambel oak (Quercus gambelii)—in May 2025 at 13 designated
locations around the perimeter of Area G. We collected four soil and two composite tree samples
at the bottom of Cañada del Buey, near the boundary between the LANL site and the Pueblo de
San Ildefonso (Figure 7-4). All samples were analyzed for tritium, americium-241, cesium-137,
plutonium-238, plutonium-239/240, strontium-90, uranium-234, uranium-235/236, and
uranium-238.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-5

Figure 7-4. Locations of soil and vegetation samples collected around Area G and near the Laboratory and Pueblo de San Ildefonso boundary in
 2025. (MDA = Material Disposal Area).

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-6

Area G Soil Results
The 2025 soil results at Area G are summarized as follows (refer to Supplemental Table S7-1 for
individual results):
 • Strontium-90 was not detected in soil around Area G.
 • Cesium-137 and uranium-235/236 were detected, but all values were below the regional
 statistical reference level.
 • Tritium was detected above the regional statistical reference level at two locations.
 • Uranium-234 and uranium-238 levels were similar to or below the regional statistical
 reference levels.
 • Americium-241, plutonium-238, and plutonium-239/240 levels were above the regional
 statistical reference levels in several locations.
 • All radionuclide levels are far below their soil ecological screening levels.

Plutonium-238 and plutonium-239/240 levels in soil samples collected on the north,
northeastern, and eastern side of Area G were above their regional statistical reference levels;
one sample collected on the south side was above the regional statistical level for plutonium-
239/240 (Table S7-1). Americium-241 levels in soil samples collected on the north, northeastern,
eastern, and south side of Area G were above its regional statistical reference level (Table S7-1).
These concentrations are similar to previous years (Figure 7-5). Americium-241 increased at
location 58-01, and plutonium-239/240 increased at location 30-01 over time (Kendall’s Tau,
p < 0.05). Trend analyses were not performed on uranium-235/236; refer to the Analytical
Laboratory Quality Assessment section in Chapter 7 of the 2024 Annual Site Environmental
Report for more information (LANL 2025). All radionuclide levels were far below their soil
ecological screening levels.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-7

Figure 7-5. (A) Americium-241; (B) plutonium-238; (C) plutonium-239/240 activities in surface soil
 samples collected from five locations on the northern, northeastern, and eastern side
              (locations 38-01, 40-01, 42-01, 45-05 and 48-01); and (D) tritium activities in surface soil

Area G Vegetation Results
Tree samples (primarily one-seed juniper, an evergreen species, and Gambel oak, a deciduous
species) were collected at the same general locations as the soil samples (Figure 7-4); however,
because of a firebreak along the fence line, some of the trees were located more than 30 feet
away from the fence around Area G, particularly on the northern and eastern sides. Trees can
acquire radionuclides either by taking them up through their root systems or by having
radioactive material land on the surfaces of leaves and branches.
The 2025 native tree results at Area G are summarized as follows (refer to Supplemental Table
S7-2 for individual results):
 • Most radionuclides in overstory vegetation samples were either not detected or were
 below the regional statistical reference levels.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-8

 • All measured radionuclide levels were below the biota dose screening levels for
 terrestrial plants.

Similar to previous years, tritium levels in overstory vegetation were highest (up to 543
picocuries per milliliter) in trees growing in the southern sections of Area G near the tritium
disposal shafts. Tritium levels are far below the biota dose screening level of 345,000 picocuries
per milliliter. The levels of plant tritium are highly variable from year to year, which could be a
result of any (or a combination) of the following: soil moisture, depth of roots, time of sampling,
distance from the perimeter fence, temperature, or barometric pressure.
Strontium-90 was detected slightly above the regional statistical reference level of 2.20
picocuries per gram in one of the overstory vegetation samples collected around the perimeter of
Area G (2.34 picocuries per gram; Table S7-2). This level is far below the biota dose screening
level of 76,444 picocuries per gram.
Americium-241 was detected above the regional statistical level of 0.019 picocuries per gram in
three of the overstory vegetation samples. Detections ranged from 0.044 to 0.201 picocuries per
gram. These values are far below the biota dose screening level of 778 picocuries per gram.
Cesium-237 and urainium-238 were detected in overstory vegetation samples. All detections
were below the regional statistical reference levels. Plutonium-238, plutonium-239/240,
uranium-234, and uranium-235/236 were not detected in any vegetation samples collected
around Area G (Table S7-2). No radionuclides were increasing in overstory vegetation collected
around Area G (Kendall’s Tau, p > 0.05). Trend analyses were not performed on uranium-
235/236; for more information, refer to the Analytical Laboratory Quality Assessment section in
Chapter 7 of the 2024 Annual Site Environmental Report (LANL 2025).

Other Samples Collected from Pueblo de San Ildefonso
In May 2025, we collected two soil samples from Pueblo de San Ildefonso: one on Pueblo de San
Ildefonso Sacred Area lands on the north side of the fence line across from Area G and the other
farther north near Tsankawi. The sample collected near Tsankawi was a mixture of soil and
sediment.
Several radionuclides were not detected in the soil samples. All detected radionuclides were
below their regional statistical reference level, and all radionuclides were well below the most
sensitive no‑effect ecological soil screening levels (Table S7‑3). No radionuclides are increasing
over time (Kendall’s Tau, p > 0.05). We did not perform trend analyses on uranium-235/236; for
more information, refer to the Analytical Laboratory Quality Assessment section in Chapter 7 of
the 2024 Annual Site Environmental Report (LANL 2025).
Most inorganic elements were detected in soil samples from Pueblo de San Ildefonso, and all
concentrations were below their regional statistical reference levels; however, similar to previous
years, manganese, mercury, selenium, and vanadium exceeded no‑effect ecological soil
screening levels in one or both of the soil sample locations (Table S7‑4). Selenium exceeded the
low-effect ecological screening level for the montane shrew at both locations, and vanadium
exceeded the low‑effect ecological soil screening level for the American Robin in a soil sample
at the Sacred Area location. The regional statistical reference levels of these elements are also
above no‑effect ecological soil screening levels (Table S7‑4).
The level of thallium is increasing over time in soil collected from the Sacred Area (Kendall’s
Tau, p < 0.05); however, thallium was not detected in 2025, and the percentage of nondetects (33
percent) could be influencing this observation. The level of cadmium is increasing over time in
soil collected near Tsankawi (Kendall’s Tau, p < 0.05). No other inorganic elements were
increasing over time in soil collected from Pueblo de San Ildefonso (Kendall’s Tau, p > 0.05). To
eliminate bias, trend analyses were conducted only on elements where the analytical method
(Inductively Coupled Plasma-Optical Emission Spectrometry [ICP-OES]) and Inductively
Coupled Plasma-Mass Spectrometry [ICP-MS]) is consistent over the time period of interest
(e.g., past 10 years). Following this guidance, trend analyses on aluminum, antimony, beryllium,
chromium, cobalt, copper, iron, magnesium, nickel, and silver were omitted; refer to the
Analytical Laboratory Quality Assessment section in this chapter.

Strontium-90 in Vegetation
In 2024, we observed strontium-90 results that were unusually large and exceeded the regional
statistical reference level in two vegetation samples collected from perimeter locations (refer to
LANL 2025 for more information). One vegetation sample was collected from Pueblo de San
Ildefonso. This sample consisted of six plant species and contained strontium-90 at 16.9

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-12

picocuries per gram; the other vegetation sample was collected from the Quemazon Trailhead
area and consisted of three plant species and contained strontium-90 at 13.8 picocuries per gram,
both of which exceeded the regional statistical reference level of 2.20 picocuries per gram.
In 2025, we further investigated strontium-90 in vegetation by resampling at Pueblo de San
Ildefonso and the Quemazon Trailhead. Our goals were to assess
 • similarities between 2024 and 2025 by repeating vegetation sampling for strontium-90 at
 the same two locations,
 • if strontium-90 concentrations varied among plant species by collecting and submitting
 individual plant species rather than composite samples, and
 • if rinsing the plants with water before submitting them for analyses impacted the
 strontium-90 results.

In 2025 at the Pueblo de San Ildefonso location, we collected six vegetation samples, and at the
Quemazon Trailhead, we collected three vegetation samples. We collected the same species of
vegetation that we collected in 2024; however, rather than combining them into a composite
sample, each species was submitted and analyzed as its own sample. Furthermore, at the
Quemazon Trailhead location, we collected three additional vegetation samples of the same
species but rinsed them with tap water before submitting them for analysis.
From the Pueblo de San Ildefonso location, three vegetation samples did not contain detectable
strontium-90 concentrations. The three samples with detectable strontium-90 ranged from 0.663
to 1.37 picocuries per gram. Strontium-90 was detected in all six samples from the Quemazon
Trailhead which ranged from 0.928 to 1.80 picocuries per gram in rinsed samples and from 1.09
to 1.74 picocuries per gram in unrinsed samples. In two vegetation samples, strontium-90 was
slightly lower in the rinsed samples when compared with the unrinsed samples, but the opposite
trend was observed in the third vegetation sample. All detectable strontium-90 concentrations
were below the regional statistical reference level of 2.20 picocuries per gram and far below the
biota dose screening level of 53,750 picocuries per gram (Table S7-5).
In summary, 9 of 12 samples contained detectable strontium-90 concentrations, and all activities
were an order of magnitude less than strontium-90 concentrations in vegetation from the same
locations in 2024, which shows inconsistency in activities between the two years. Plant species
also did not appear to influence the strontium-90 concentrations observed. Lastly, although
rinsing the vegetation samples could have helped to distinguish if strontium-90 was deposited on
the surface of the plants from dust or soil versus plant root uptake, we did not observe consistent
trends where rinsed samples contained less strontium-90.

Dual-Axis Radiographic Hydrodynamic Test Facility at Technical Area 15
The Dual-Axis Radiographic Hydrodynamic Test Facility is used to study properties of the
explosives that trigger nuclear weapons. We monitor soil, sediment from local drainages, plants,
and animals to determine if constituents released from the facility could be affecting plants or
animals and if observed levels are consistent with our expectations of radionuclide and chemical
uptake. This environmental monitoring has occurred annually since 1996. The Dual-Axis
Radiographic Hydrodynamic Test Facility began firing-site operations in 2000. The types of
mitigations used to control releases from detonations at the facility have changed over time.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-13

Biota or products of biota collected around the Dual-Axis Radiographic Hydrodynamic Test
Facility have included overstory vegetation, small mammals, honeybees, honey, bird eggs, and
bird nestlings. We rotate the collection of vegetation, honey, and small mammals on a three-year
cycle. Bird samples are collected opportunistically when abandoned or infertile eggs or deceased
nestlings are found in local nest boxes.
In 2025, we collected soil, sediment, honey, and bird egg samples at the facility. Radionuclide
and chemical levels were not detected at concentrations detrimental to human health or to the
environment. Refer to LANL (2026) for soil, sediment, and honey results and Stanek et al.
(2026) for bird results.

Open-Detonation and Open-Burn Firing Sites
In 2025, nonviable bird eggs and a nonviable nestling were opportunistically collected from
Technical Area 16 Burn Grounds and were analyzed for per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances
(PFAS). PFAS are synthetic compounds that have been produced since the 1940s and are found
in many manufactured items such as cookware, food packaging, cosmetics, stain repellents,
semiconductors, lubricants, textiles, paints, and fire-fighting foams (Phong Vo et al. 2020,
Gaines 2023). PFAS compounds repel oil, stains, grease, and water and are fire resistant.
Most PFAS compounds were either not detected or were below the regional statistical reference
levels. Refer to Stanek et al. (2026) for results.

Sediment and Flood Retention Structures
Many chemicals and radionuclides released into the environment adhere to soil and sediment
particles. Stormwater flows can transport these soil and sediment particles downstream in
canyons. We have constructed flood and sediment retention structures to reduce flood risks and
to stop or slow the movement of sediments and associated chemicals and radionuclides off the
LANL site.
The Los Alamos Canyon weir and the Pajarito Canyon flood retention structure were built
following the Cerro Grande Fire in 2000. As part of an environmental analysis of actions taken
in response to the Cerro Grande Fire, DOE identified various measures to minimize impacts that
resulted from the fire (DOE 2000). One of the measures is monitoring soil, surface water,
groundwater, and biota upstream of flood-control structures; within sediment-retention basins;
and within sediment traps to determine if constituent concentrations in these areas adversely
affect plants or animals.
We collect native grasses, forbs, and small mammals in the retention basins of the Los Alamos
Canyon weir and the Pajarito Canyon flood retention structure for monitoring purposes. We aim
to collect the following samples:
 • a composite understory vegetation sample for radionuclide, inorganic element, and PFAS
 analyses;
 • a composite sample of approximately 100 grams of whole-body small mammals for
 radionuclide analyses;
 • three individual small mammals for inorganic elements analyses;
 • three individual small mammals for polychlorinated biphenyl (PCB) analysis; and

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-14

 • three individual small mammals for PFAS analysis.

Los Alamos Canyon Weir
The Los Alamos Canyon weir is made of rock-filled wire cages called gabions and is designed to
slow water flow and reduce the movement of sediment off Laboratory property. The weir was
built in Los Alamos Canyon near the northeastern boundary of the Laboratory site. The retention
basin upstream of the weir covers more than 1 acre. Accumulated sediment was excavated from
the retention basin in 2009, 2011, 2013, and 2014. Sediment excavated in 2009 was placed on
the west side of the basin and stabilized, whereas sediment excavated in 2011, 2013, and 2014
was analyzed, placed on a plastic liner, contained within a berm, compacted, and seeded
approximately 0.5 miles west of the weir in Los Alamos Canyon.
We did not collect understory vegetation or small mammals from the Los Alamos Canyon weir
structure in 2025; these sampling events are now on a three-year basis rather than on an annual
basis. See Gaukler et al. (2024) for more information.

Pajarito Canyon Flood Retention Structure
The Pajarito Canyon flood retention structure is located upstream of Technical Area 18. The
structure extends 390 feet across the canyon and is about 70 feet high. The bottom of the
retention structure is equipped with one 42-inch-diameter drainage culvert that allows
stormwater to drain. Accumulated water is retained behind the retention structure for no longer
than 96 hours; water drains into the existing streambed.
We no longer monitor understory vegetation or small mammals at the Pajarito Canyon flood
retention structure. See Gaukler et al. (2024) for more information.

Institutional Monitoring for Radionuclides and Chemicals
Large-Animal Monitoring Methods
We have collected tissue samples from road-killed mule deer (Odocoileus hemionus) and elk
(Cervus canadensis) from onsite, perimeter, and background locations since the 1970s (LASL
1973). In 2015, we began collecting samples from more species, including mountain lion (Puma
concolor), bobcat (Lynx rufus), black bear (Ursus americanus), coyote (Canis latrans), gray fox
(Urocyon cinereoargenteus), great horned owl (Bubo virginianus), western screech owl
(Megascops kennicottii), red-tailed hawk (Buteo jamaiciensis), gopher snake (Pituophis
catenifer), and additional species killed by vehicles or other accidents.
We consider all samples collected within the Valles Caldera National Preserve as background
and include them in the regional statistical reference levels even if they are within 9 miles of the
site’s boundary. We made this decision because the Valles Caldera is upstream and upwind of
the LANL site (considering the predominant wind direction in the region) and it does not have
industrial or urban development. Additionally, we consider all samples collected from Cochiti
Pueblo as perimeter—even though it is more than 9 miles away—because it is located
downstream of the LANL site. Results from samples collected from background locations that
are included in the regional statistical reference level calculations are not individually reported in
the supplemental tables.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-15

Here, we report results from an elk, a mule deer, a feral bull (Bos taurus), a coyote, a black bear,
a gray fox, and two gopher snakes. We collected animals from onsite and perimeter locations in
2025 (Figure 7-7). Most animals collected were casualties from vehicle strikes.
We harvested muscle and bone samples from the elk, deer, cow, coyote, black bear, and gray
fox. We analyzed bone for radionuclides and muscle for radionuclides, inorganic elements, and
PCBs or PFAS or both. We harvested muscle and whole-body samples from the gopher snakes
and analyzed the muscle samples for PCBs or PFAS or both. We analyzed the remaining bodies
for radionuclides and inorganic elements. When possible, we harvested a liver sample from each
collection and analyzed the sample for PFAS.

S7-13 for individual results):
 • Most radionuclide results fell into one or more of the following categories: not detected,
 below the regional statistical reference level, or below the biota dose screening level.
 • Most inorganic element concentrations were below their regional statistical reference
 levels.
 • PCBs were detected in most samples, and most PCB concentrations were below the
 regional statistical reference level. Deer and elk PCB levels were also far below the U.S.
 Food and Drug Administration red meat consumption guidelines.
 • Most PFAS compounds were not detected. The concentrations of most detected
 compounds are within the range reported in published literature for animal tissues
 collected from nonpolluted sites.

Several inorganic elements were not detected in elk, deer, or cow samples. Most inorganic
elements that were detected in elk, deer, and cow were below their regional statistical reference
levels. Antimony (0.816 milligrams per kilogram) slightly exceeded the regional statistical
reference level of 0.706 milligrams per kilogram, and manganese (0.536 milligrams per
kilogram) exceeded the regional statistical reference level of 0.281 milligrams per kilogram in
the deer sample (Table S7-8).

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-17

Most inorganic elements in the coyote, black bear, gray fox, and the gopher snake samples were
either not detected or were below their regional statistical reference levels. We did not detect
arsenic, beryllium, cadmium, lead, nickel, selenium, silver, or thallium in any samples.
Antimony and vanadium were higher than the regional statistical reference level in one gopher
snake sample, and antimony and mercury were higher than the regional statistical reference level
in the coyote sample (Table S7-9). As previously mentioned, we need more data from
background locations to make robust assessments for these species.

PCB Results in Large Animals
PCBs were detected in the elk and deer muscle samples; however, PCBs were not detected in the
cow muscle samples (Table S7-10). PCBs were detected in the muscle sample collected from the
deer at 0.0000292 milligrams per kilogram, exceeding the regional statistic level of 0.0000081
milligrams per kilogram (Table S7-10). However, all observations are well below the U.S. Food
and Drug Administration standard of 3 milligrams per kilogram for red meat consumption by
humans (USFDA 1987).
PCBs were detected in the coyote, black bear, and gray fox (Table S7-11); however, total PCBs
for all samples were below the regional statistical reference levels, when available (Table S7-11).

PFAS Results in Large Animals
We submitted samples to be tested for 39 PFAS compounds from an elk, a deer, a cow, a coyote,
a black bear, a gray fox, and two gopher snakes. We collected a muscle sample from all animals
and a corresponding liver sample from four of those animals.
No PFAS compounds were observed in the elk muscle sample. Perfluorooctanesulfonic acid was
detected in the deer muscle sample at 0.15 nanograms per gram (Table S7-12). We did not
collect liver samples from elk or deer. Perfluorooctanesulfonic acid was detected in the muscle
sample collected from the cow at 0.120 nanograms per gram. Three PFAS compounds were also
observed in the cow liver samples, including perfluorodecanoic acid, perfluorononanoic acid,
and perfluorooctanesulfonic acid (Table S7-12). Currently, there are no regional statistical
reference levels for deer or cow PFAS concentrations for comparisons.
A coyote muscle sample collected from a perimeter location contained three detectable PFAS
compounds, including perfluorohexanesulfonic acid, perfluorononanoic acid, and perfluoro-
octanesulfonic acid (Table S7-13). The highest concentration observed in the coyote was 0.170
nanograms per gram of perfluorooctanesulfonic acid. Currently, we have only one coyote from a
background location for comparison; therefore, no regional statistical reference levels can be
calculated.
A black bear muscle sample collected from LANL property contained four detectable PFAS
compounds, including perfluorohexanesulfonic acid, perfluorononanoic acid, perfluorooctane-
sulfonic acid, and perfluorotridecanoic acid (Table S7-13). The highest concentration observed
in the black bear sample was 0.590 nanograms per gram of perfluorooctanesulfonic acid.
Currently, no regional statistical reference levels are available for black bears.
A gray fox muscle and liver sample were collected from LANL property. Multiple PFAS
compounds were detected in both the muscle and liver samples (Table S7-13). Within the muscle
sample, perfluorohexanesulfonic acid, perfluorononanoic acid, and perfluorooctanesulfonic acid
were detected (Table S7-13). The liver sample collected from the gray fox general had higher

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-18

concentrations of detectable PFAS compounds and included the detection of 10 PFAS
compounds. The highest detected PFAS compound from any of the samples was observed in the
fox liver sample and was perfluorooctanesulfonic acid at 5.30 nanograms per gram (Table S7-13).
Two gopher snakes were collected from LANL property and contained PFAS compounds. The
gopher snake muscle samples contained only perfluorooctanesulfonic acid at 0.380 and 0.100
nanograms per gram (Table S7-13), and the liver collected from one of the gopher snakes
contained perfluorobutanoic acid, perfluorononanoic acid, and perfluorooctanesulfonic acid at
1.40, 0.160, and 0.370 nanograms per gram, respectively (Table S7-13). Currently, there are no
regional statistical reference levels for gopher snake PFAS concentrations for comparison.
Perfluorooctanesulfonic acid was the most frequently detected PFAS compound for both
ungulates and non-ungulates. Most of our observations are within the ranges of PFAS
concentrations observed in animal tissues from published studies that occurred away from
point-source pollution, including in the Antarctic, where global fallout is the primary source of
PFAS in the environment (Aas et al. 2014, Bossi et al. 2015). When liver and muscle samples
were taken from the same animal, PFAS compounds typically occurred more frequently and had
higher concentrations in liver samples, which is similar to findings in other published studies
(Robuck et al. 2021, Draghi et al. 2024). Our results also suggest that lower concentrations are
found in herbivores, such as deer and elk, compared with carnivores; however, our sample sizes
are still small, and we cannot draw robust conclusions at this time. Because PFAS are recently
emerging chemicals of concern, little is known about wildlife tissue concentrations and their
relation to adverse effects.
