Overall, most PFAS compounds were not detected in soil, sediment, or animals. Perfluorodeca-

Perfluorooctanesulfonic acid was the most commonly detected PFAS compound in animals. This
type of PFAS chemical has a longer chain structure, which means that it is more likely to build
up in the tissues of animals because it is hard for their bodies to break it down and get rid of it. In

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-19

general, we found that liver samples had a higher number of detectable PFAS compounds than
muscle samples or whole-body samples.
The concentrations of detected PFAS compounds were generally within the range of global
observations of concentrations in soil and animals collected from nonpolluted sites (Aas et al.
2014, Bossi et al. 2015, Brusseau et al. 2020). For most of our samples, we suspect that the
PFAS concentrations observed are due to nonpoint source pollution. We are exploring potential
sources for some of the PFAS compounds detected in the different sample types and locations.

Biota Radiation Dose Assessment

The purpose of the biota dose assessment is to ensure that plant and animal populations are
protected from effects of radioactive materials released from past or current site operations, as
required by DOE Order 458.1. This assessment follows the guidance of the DOE standard, A
Graded Approach for Evaluating Radiation Doses to Aquatic and Terrestrial Biota, DOE-STD-
1153-2019 (DOE 2019), and uses the standard DOE dose calculation program, RESRAD BIOTA
version 1.8.
Previous biota dose assessments reported in past annual site environmental reports concluded
that biota doses for populations at the Laboratory site were well below the DOE limits of 1 rad
per day for terrestrial plants and aquatic animals and 0.1 rad per day for terrestrial animals (DOE
2019).
Plants and animals receive doses from external radiation. Plants receive internal doses from
radionuclides taken up through their roots. Animals receive internal doses when they eat plants.
When a predator eats its prey, there is a possibility for bioaccumulation 17 as the ingested material
passes up the food chain. Bioaccumulation is measured with “bioaccumulation factors” or
“concentration ratios,” which are the ratios of the levels of radionuclides in living tissue to the
levels in the local soil and water.
Published concentration ratios allow us to estimate the levels of radionuclides in living tissue
from the levels in soil. The biota doses reported in the following paragraphs are calculated using
site representative values as described in Appendix F of DOE-STD-1153-2019 (DOE 2019).
Whenever the data allow calculation of the dose from either soil or tissue data, the largest dose is
reported.
The material that potentially contributes to the biota doses at the LANL site is legacy waste
material. Ongoing remediation and radioactive decay result in decreasing radionuclide activity
levels over time, so a decreasing trend in biota dose is expected. However, ongoing operations
and movement of soil or sediment could cause an accumulation of radioactive material, so key
locations are reassessed annually.

17 Bioaccumulation is the gradual accumulation of constituents, such as a chemical or radionuclide, in a living organism over

  time.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-20

In the following sections, we calculate the worst-case biota doses for plants and animals at
Material Disposal Area G, at the Dual‑Axis Radiographic Hydrodynamic Test Facility, at the
Los Alamos Canyon weir, and at the Pajarito Canyon flood‑retention structure.

Mesa-Top Facilities

Area G
This chapter reports new measurements of soil and vegetation around Material Disposal Area G,
known as Area G. The data, listed in supplementary Tables S7-1 and S7-2, are generally
comparable with previous years although some year-to year variation occurs depending on the
exact locations sampled. This year, the largest variation is for tritium, which is called “H-3” in
RESRAD-BIOTA.
The biota dose caused by tritium varies as a function of both time and location. The uptake of
tritiated water into plants varies with time depending on several variables: the ambient
temperatures of the soil, air, and vegetation; the moisture from intermittent rain or snow; and the
daily and annual growth cycles. It also varies with location depending on the distance from the
perimeter fence and which roots are in contact with buried waste, as well as the concentration
and containment of the waste. A more realistic assessment of tritium dose would use averages of
several locations and several times instead of the single worst-case value used here.
As recommended by the DOE standard (DOE 2019), the following assessments use the highest
measured activity levels. We report the Area G results in Table 7-2 and Table 7-3. The largest
tritium concentrations are located near the tritium burial shafts, which are near locations 29-03
and 30-1, as indicated in Figure 7-5.

The largest dose contribution at the Dual‑Axis Radiographic Hydrodynamic Test Facility is from
uranium, most of which is depleted uranium from Laboratory site operations. The levels of the
other radionuclides are consistent with natural background and global fallout. Table 7-4 and
Table 7-5 show that the biota doses are well below the DOE limits of 0.1 rad per day for animals
and 1 rad per day for plants. No impacts are expected to biota health.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-23

Sediment Retention Sites in Canyons

Los Alamos Canyon Weir
The Los Alamos Canyon weir receives drainage from former Technical Areas 01, 02, and 21.
The soil and sediment trapped by the weir include slightly elevated activities of fission products
(cesium-137 and strontium-90) and transuranic radionuclides (americium and plutonium).
Tritium and uranium concentrations are consistent with natural background. As noted previously
in section Los Alamos Canyon Weir, understory vegetation and small mammal samples were not
collected in 2025. Sampling of the Los Alamos Canyon weir is now on a rotating three-year
basis with the most recent sampling campaign in 2024. Data are reported in Table S8.1 (sample
SFB-25-372968). Concentrations were less than those reported at this location in 2024.

Pajarito Canyon Flood Retention Structure
The Pajarito Canyon flood retention structure does not receive significant quantities of
Laboratory site radionuclides, and any contribution from DOE operations was indistinguishable
from background levels. During 2025, no samples were collected at this location. The total biota
dose in Pajarito Canyon is less than 1 percent of the DOE limits and has no expected impact on
biota health.

Large Animals
Whenever possible, we take samples from animals killed on the roads and analyze for
radionuclides. As shown in Tables S7-6 and S7‑7 and discussed in sections Large-Animal
Monitoring Methods and Large-Animal Monitoring Results, in 2025, these samples included
deer, elk, a cow, a coyote, a bear, a fox, and a snake. The results were consistent with natural
background and global fallout concentrations (Ryti et al. 1998) and do not cause significant biota
dose.

Previous biota dose assessments have shown that biota doses at the Laboratory site are far below
the DOE limits. This 2025 assessment confirms the previous assessments and shows that no
harmful effects to the health of biota populations are expected from Laboratory-sourced
radioactive materials.

Biological Resources Management Program
As discussed in Chapter 3, we monitor federally listed threatened and endangered species and
migratory birds; provide guidelines and requirements for site operations to minimize impacts to
sensitive species and their habitats, including those proposed and petitioned to be listed under the
Endangered Species Act; and ensure that all operations comply with federal and state
regulations.

Threatened and Endangered Species
In 2025, we completed surveys for three species protected under the Endangered Species Act:
the Mexican spotted owl (Strix occidentalis lucida), the southwestern willow flycatcher
(Empidonax traillii extimus), and the Jemez Mountains salamander (Plethodon neomexicanus).

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-24

Mexican Spotted Owl
The Mexican spotted owl generally inhabits mixed conifer, ponderosa pine (Pinus ponderosa),
and Gambel oak (Quercus gambelii) forests in mountains and canyons (U.S. Fish and Wildlife
Service 2012). Mexican spotted owls in the Jemez Mountains of northern New Mexico prefer
cliff faces in canyons for their nest sites (Johnson and Johnson 1985). As part of the Laboratory’s
Threatened and Endangered Species Habitat Management Plan, we have identified Mexican
spotted owl habitat based on a combination of cliff habitat and forest characteristics (LANL
2022). In 2025, we detected Mexican spotted owls in the Cañon de Valle, Mortandad, Threemile,
and Pajarito canyon habitat areas. We confirmed that breeding pairs occupied both the
Mortandad and Pajarito sites, successfully fledging two and three young, respectively. Mexican
spotted owls have occupied the Mortandad site in previous years (Thompson et al. 2023), but the
Pajarito Canyon is a newly occupied nesting location. The detections in Cañon de Valle and
Threemile could have been a wandering male because we were unable to confirm occupancy.
In addition to annual surveys, we deployed automated recording units in Mexican spotted owl
habitat to supplement field surveys and assess year-round use from 2022 to 2025 in Los Alamos,
Threemile, and Mortandad canyons. Positive detections—primarily males—were recorded at
each location through automated recording unit monitoring and subsequently confirmed through
on-the-ground surveys. The effectiveness of automated recording units in detecting owls without
the use of call-playback surveys led to their expanded deployment across canyons. Notably, in
2025 an automated recording unit recorded both a male and female in previously unoccupied
Pajarito Canyon before the breeding season; follow-up surveys confirmed breeding and three
nestlings in July 2025.

Assessing Mexican Spotted Owl Productivity Overtime
To evaluate the effectiveness of LANL’s Habitat Management Plan on Mexican spotted owl
productivity, we analyzed survey data from 1994 through 2025, measuring productivity as the
number of young fledged per year. We included the influence of drought on productivity for this
analysis, given evidence that spring drought severity can negatively affect avian reproduction.
We used R statistical software version 4.5.3 for all data analyses (R Core Team 2026). We
modeled Mexican spotted owl productivity (number of young fledged) using generalized linear
mixed models with a negative binomial error distribution to account for overdispersion. We
included year and Palmer Drought Severity Index as fixed effects to evaluate temporal trends and
the influence of drought on productivity. The Palmer Drought Severity Index is a standardized
measure of moisture conditions, where negative values indicate drought and positive values
indicate wetter than average conditions; values near zero represent normal conditions. We used
the Palmer Drought Severity Index for north-central New Mexico, averaged from December
through May to reflect breeding-season conditions. Pair identity was included as a random effect
to account for repeated observations of territorial pairs. Continuous predictors were standardized
to improve model convergence and interpretability.
Results indicated a positive trend in productivity across years. This relationship was statistically
significant at the α = 0.05 level and therefore indicates a stable population trend for Mexican
spotted owls on site (β = 0.474 ± 0.152, p = 0.002; Figure 7-8). Visualizations of annual fledging
totals across five different pairs at monitoring sites (Pajarito, Mortandad-1, Mortandad-2, Cañon
de Valle, and Threemile) showed year-to-year variability, with some increases following the
implementation of the Habitat Management Plan in the year 2000 (Figure 7-9).

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-25

Figure 7-8. Temporal trend in Mexican spotted owl productivity. Points show observed fledgling counts,
 and the solid line represents model-predicted productivity (±95% CI) from a negative
 binomial mixed-effects model including pair as a random effect (β = 0.474 ± 0.152, p =
 0.002). The vertical dashed line marks implementation of the Habitat Management Plan in
 2000. Note: HMP = Habitat Management Plan.

Figure 7-9. A stacked bar graph of fledged young from 1995 to 2025 by site, with the year of LANL’s
 Habitat Management Plan implementation (2000) shown as a dashed line. Note: CDV =
 Cañon de Valle.

Additionally, productivity was strongly influenced by climatic conditions, with higher
productivity observed in wetter years (β = 0.691 ± 0.177, p = 0.001), indicating that wetter
conditions were associated with greater reproductive success (Figure 7-10).

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-26

Figure 7-10. Relationship between drought conditions and Mexican spotted owl productivity. Points show
 observed fledgling counts, and the solid line represents model-predicted productivity (±95%
 CI) from a negative binomial mixed-effects model including pair as a random effect (β =
 0.691 ± 0.177, p = 0.001). Negative Palmer Drought Severity Index values indicate drought,
 whereas positive values indicate wetter conditions.

Overall, these findings suggest that the Habitat Management Plan has helped maintain stable
Mexican spotted owl productivity on site since its implementation in 2000, with increases in
successful fledging events over time; however, reproductive success remains strongly influenced
by climatic conditions, particularly drought severity. Future management should therefore
continue protections under the Habitat Management Plan while incorporating adaptive strategies,
such as maintaining structural forest complexity and canopy cover, supporting water availability,
and implementing fire mitigation treatments that preserve key habitat features to buffer the
species against increasing drought variability and stand-replacing wildfire risk. As the
Laboratory prepares to update the Habitat Management Plan in the coming years, building on the
Plan’s demonstrated successes will be essential to ensure continued effective management of this
species on site by balancing habitat protection with the growing need for proactive fire
management and increased development.

Jemez Mountains Salamander
The Jemez Mountains salamander occurs predominantly at elevations between 7,000 and 11,000
feet in mixed-conifer and spruce-fir forests (Degenhardt et al. 1996). Under the Habitat
Management Plan, we have identified Jemez Mountains salamander habitat based on a
geographical information systems analysis and a field-validated inspection of areas with suitable
habitat components (LANL 2022). Currently, five Jemez Mountains salamander habitat areas
exist at the Laboratory site in four canyons. We conduct surveys in these areas when a specific
project need occurs and when suitable environmental conditions are met.
In 2025, biologists conducted surveys within and surrounding the proposed footprint of a
wildland-fire thinning project in Los Alamos Canyon. Jemez Mountains salamanders have
historically occupied some areas within the project footprint; therefore, we focused surveys
within those areas. The project underwent formal Section 7 consultation and received a
biological opinion in 2025 (LANL 2024c). The goal of the consultation is to address the potential
direct, indirect, and cumulative effects on federally listed threatened and endangered species

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-27

caused by disturbance and habitat alteration from fuels mitigation treatment in and along Los
Alamos Canyon. We found no salamanders during surveys in these areas in 2025.

Southwestern Willow Flycatcher
The southwestern willow flycatcher breeds in dense riparian habitats composed of native or
nonnative shrubs and small trees, typically near surface water or saturated soils. Suitable habitat
is characterized by high stem density and structurally complex vegetation, often dominated by
willows (Salix sp.) or mixed riparian species, and could include tamarisk (Tamarix sp.) or other
nonnative plants (USFWS 2002, 2025). Under the Habitat Management Plan (LANL 2022), only
one area has been historically identified as habitat for the southwestern willow flycatcher: in the
bottom of Pajarito Canyon. There were no detections during surveys in 2025.

Yellow-Billed Cuckoo
The yellow-billed cuckoo (Coccyzus americanus) is strongly associated with riparian habitats in
the western United States and typically nests in low- to mid-elevation river corridors, most
commonly in areas dominated by cottonwoods and willows (Halterman et al. 2015), though they
can use a variety of dense riparian and adjacent woodland vegetation types for nesting habitat
(Stanek et al. 2021, Beauregard 2024). Potential habitat on Laboratory property for this species is
located along the Rio Grande; there are no current operations in this area. No breeding habitat is
identified for the species under the Habitat Management Plan (LANL 2022).
We do not conduct surveys every year, but we review any work activities that could affect
habitat for this species (Keller 2015). The Electrical Power Capacity Upgrade project will cross
the Rio Grande. No surveys or automated recording unit deployments were conducted in 2025.

Species Proposed or Petitioned for Listing

Pinyon Jay Monitoring
We initiated a sitewide pilot study in late 2022 to monitor pinyon jays (Gymnorhinus
cyanocephalus) at the Laboratory. Pinyon jays are highly associated with woodlands dominated
by piñon pines (Pinus edulis) and one-seed juniper (Juniperus monosperma). Although this
species is not federally protected under the Endangered Species Act, it has been petitioned to be
listed and is currently under review (USFWS 2023).
We have been monitoring pinyon jays on LANL property using autonomous recording units
since 2022 and performing ground surveys since 2023. For ground surveys, we follow the
Pinyon Jay Working Group survey protocol (Boone et al. 2023) in coordination with other
statewide surveys. From March through April 2025, we conducted 12 pinyon jay ground surveys
with no detections. We also placed autonomous recording units in three locations in Technical
Areas 54, 49, and 71. We recorded two positive detections in Technical Area 54.

Monarch Butterfly Monitoring
In December 2014, the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service proposed listing the monarch butterfly
(Danaus plexippus) as a threatened species under the Endangered Species Act (USFWS 2024).
Although the agency determined that the species meets the criteria for listing due to significant
population declines, a final rule has not yet been issued. Since 2018, we have documented
monarch observations, timing of the monarch life cycle, and milkweed locations on site (LANL
2024b). In 2025, we documented 13 monarch caterpillars. We will continue to identify and

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-28

document onsite monarch observations and milkweed locations to help minimize impacts to the
species when feasible. These efforts will better inform management and enable operations if this
species is federally listed in the future.

Migratory Bird Monitoring

Bird Banding Mark-Recapture Studies
We have operated a breeding-season bird-banding station in the Sandia Canyon wetland since
2014. This wetland contains primarily broadleaf cattail (Typha latifolia), lanceleaf cottonwood
(Populus acuminata), narrowleaf willow (Salix exigua), and Russian olive (Elaeagnus
angustifolia) (N3B 2019). Beginning in May each year, we operate the bird-banding station
using a national protocol called Monitoring Avian Productivity and Survivorship (DeSante et al.
2021). By following a standardized protocol, we produce data that can be compared among sites.
Since 2014, we have captured 2,394 birds that represent 84 species. In 2025, we captured 185
birds that represented 37 species. The most-captured species in 2025 was the pygmy nuthatch
(Sitta pygmaea).
We also operate a bird-banding station on Laboratory property during fall migration in a wetland
and riparian complex in Technical Area 36 on the north side of Pajarito Road. Since 2010, we
have captured 6,503 birds at the fall banding site that represented 95 species. In 2025, we
captured 340 birds that represented 45 species. The most-captured bird species at this site in
2025 was the orange-crowned warbler (Leiothlypis celata).
In 2025, we supported a research project within the avian nestbox network to evaluate post-
fledging survival of western bluebirds. We deployed 25 very-high-frequency avian transmitters
to monitor post-fledgling survival of Western Bluebird nestlings across four sites that
represented a range of elevational and habitat characteristics. This technology sends out a
frequency that biologists can detect with a receiver providing location data for each individual
bird. The study is intended to assess environmental and habitat-related factors that influence
juvenile survival. Data analysis is currently underway as part of a master’s thesis, and results will
be reported in the 2026 Annual Site Environmental Report.
More information about bird-banding methods and annual results can be found in Stanek and
Hathcock (2019).

Bird Monitoring at Open-Detonation and Open-Burn Firing Sites
In 2013, we began documenting bird populations at two open-detonation sites, an open-burn site,
and three control areas using point-count surveys and nestbox monitoring. We test explosives at
open-detonation sites, where materials are ignited for self-sustained combustion (for example, to
remove residues of high explosives) at the open-burn site. The two open-detonation sites are
Minie Site at Technical Area 36 and Point 6 at Technical Area 39; the open-burn site is in
Technical Area 16. The project’s objective is to determine if operations at these sites impact bird
species richness (the number of different species present), species diversity (a combination of the
number of species present and their relative abundance), or composition (the presence or absence
of each individual species). Analyses of this long-term dataset indicate that operations at the
three sites are not negatively affecting local bird populations. Refer to Stanek et al. (2026) for a
detailed presentation and discussion of results.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-29

Implementing Migratory Bird Best Management Practices
Efforts to protect migratory birds on site include implementing best management practices from
LANL’s Migratory Bird Management Plan (LANL 2024d) to help meet requirements of the
Migratory Bird Treaty Act. One of these initiatives is to reduce the number of bird-window
collisions—when birds collide with glass—an overlooked but devastating threat that kills up to
one billion birds annually in the United States (Loss et al. 2014, Kornreich et al. 2024). Even this
staggering figure is likely an underestimate because roughly one in four collisions leaves no
visible evidence on windows (Klem 2009). These strikes happen because birds mistake
reflections in glass for open sky or habitat. The sheer scale of this mortality raises conservation
concerns, especially for species already in decline. Simple fixes, such as window deterrents with
a 2-by-2-inch dotted pattern that runs horizontally and vertically, can make a meaningful
difference (Klem 2025).
To address this, we proposed a pilot project that was included in and funded by an environmental
action plan (refer to section Environmental Management System in Chapter 3). In 2025, a high-
incidence area at the Technical Area 03 Otowi Cafeteria was fitted with Feather Friendly dot
patterns, which are expected to reduce collisions by 90–95 percent, and no strikes have been
reported since installation. Outreach efforts, including staff reporting tools, presentations, and
onsite decals, have also helped raise awareness and support bird-safe practices across the
Laboratory.

Riparian Monitoring
Feral cattle (Bos taurus) have been documented along the DOE/National Nuclear Security
Administration–owned section of the Rio Grande Corridor of White Rock Canyon in Los
Alamos County, New Mexico, since the 1970s (NPS 1994). Feral cattle activity is known to be
especially impactful to riparian ecosystems, which are hubs for biodiversity and provide
resources for multiple endangered species including the southwestern willow flycatcher and
yellow-billed cuckoo on LANL property (Poessel et al. 2020, Sanchez 2021). In 2023, the
National Nuclear Security Administration directed Triad to develop an implementation plan for
the removal of feral cattle on DOE property (Stanek et al. 2024). The plan was finalized in 2023.
In 2025, DOE had further interest in chronicling the effects of feral cattle presence, and Triad
conducted data-collection efforts.
In September 2025, we conducted a month-long vegetation-monitoring project to gain a
preliminary understanding of the status of riparian ecosystems within White Rock Canyon and
the impacts of feral cattle as of winter 2025. We used riparian vegetation-monitoring protocols
and rapid assessments to quantify the intensity and extent of feral cattle impacts on riparian
ecosystems.
The main area of interest for the study was the corridor of White Rock Canyon within the
boundaries of Technical Areas 33 and 70, which previously had the most cattle activity (Sanchez
2021). We established 12 plots that comprised 3 plot types: 4 plots along the bank of the Rio
Grande (Streambank), 4 plots near spring locations (Spring), and 4 plots on upland locations
(Upland; Figure 7-11). Upland locations consisted of areas with no immediate river access, such
as benches higher up the side of the canyon.
Plot layouts were adapted from the Assessment, Inventory, and Monitoring (AIM) National
Aquatic Monitoring Framework. Each plot was composed of three staggered, 20-meter transects

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-30

that run parallel to each other, 20 meters apart, and perpendicular to the Rio Grande (BLM
2024). Line-point intercept data were taken every 1 meter along each of the three transects.
Vegetation class and species were recorded, along with any non-living litter.
To record evidence of cattle disturbances, we recorded scope and intensity of various
disturbances within each plot according to Rapid Assessment of Cattle Impacts in Riparian
Exclosures developed by the Center for Biological Diversity (CBD 2018). We visually assessed
the area and accounted for six different types of cattle impact categories (grazing evidence,
browse pressure, ground cover disturbance intensity and extent, and streambank degradation
intensity and extent) and noted the disturbance on a scale of 1–4. Impact categories were then
averaged for each plot to determine an overall impact level (light, moderate, or significant).
Statistical analyses were conducted in R—a type of statistical computing and data analysis
programming language—to determine plant community differences among plots (R Core Team
2026). We performed a biodiversity analysis to determine differences in plant composition to
evaluate the difference in abundance and diversity of native versus nonnative plant species
among the different plot types. We tested for significant results using the nonparametric Kruskal-
Wallace test by ranks because the data were non-normal using the dplyr package in R (Wickham
et al. 2026).
Native plants were more abundant than nonnative species in the Spring (p < 0.001) and Upland
(p < 0.005) plot types. The Spring plots had the least amount of cattle impact (Table 7-6, Figure
7-11) and the highest proportion of native vegetation. Of the 12 plots, 8 had significant overall
signs of cattle damage (Table 7-6, Figure 7-11). This damage was consistent throughout plots,
showing an abundance of tracks, trails, wallows, and browse pressure on native woody
regeneration.

Table 7-6. Cattle Impact Assessment for Riparian Monitoring across Three Habitat Types in White
 Rock Canyon
 Ground Disturbance Streambank Degradation
 Overall
 Location Grazing Browse Intensity Extent Intensity Extent Impact
Streambank Significant Significant Significant Significant Significant Moderate Significant
Spring Light Light Moderate Moderate Moderate Moderate Moderate
Upland Significant Significant Significant Significant N/A N/A Significant
Total Significant Significant Significant Significant Significant Moderate Significant

The data collected is a baseline for comparison following the proposed managed removal of feral
cattle from the canyon. Within the riparian ecosystem, we noted indicators of current and heavy
use by feral cattle, including multiple wallowing areas where soils were completely denuded of
vegetation and highly compacted. Based on the severity and extent of impacts observed in these
areas, cattle use appears consistent and ongoing over an extended period of multiple years,
supporting the need for removal and managed restoration.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-31

Figure 7-11. Plot locations and their corresponding overall impact levels based on the rapid assessments
 of cattle damage in White Rock Canyon.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-32

Forest Monitoring Program
The Forest Monitoring Program monitors forest management treatments—including forest
thinning—that allow us to assess our effectiveness at reducing fuels, restoring forests for
improved forest resiliency, and protecting threatened and endangered species habitat (LANL
2019).

Monitoring and Documentation of Forest Management Activities
Our monitoring results allow assessment and adaptive management for the following objectives:
 • Implement treatments to manage vegetative communities for resilience, including fire-
 related disturbances
 • Protect habitat for federally listed threatened and endangered species
 • Minimize soil erosion and offsite sediment transport
 • Assess effectiveness of fuel treatments
 • Increase forest resilience to drought and fire (achieve more water availability to
 individual trees and shrubs by establishing lower tree densities, increased water
 infiltration, and slower water runoff)
 • Establish a mosaic forest structure in both space and time (for example, treatments will
 be implemented over several years, with spatial gaps between heavily treated areas)
 • Increase adequate forest gaps and openings to increase available light to and diversity of
 understory herbaceous vegetation
 • Stop the spread of invasive plant species, including Siberian elm (Ulmus pumila),
 Russian olive, common teasel (Dipsacus fullonum), and invasive thistles (Cirsium spp.)
 • Preserve the oldest ponderosa and piñon pine individuals for their genetic and habitat
 importance
 • Limit the spread of damaging insects
 • Improve riparian ecosystem function (for example, increase cover of native riparian
 vegetation and reduce channel downcutting, thereby improving access of water to
 floodplain)

Piñon Monitoring
In 2024 and 2025, we collected data on the abundance of piñon pine trees in piñon-juniper
ecosystems that were undergoing forest-thinning operations. Piñon-juniper ecosystems provide
habitat for several species, including the pinyon jay. To document piñon cone abundance, we
mapped locations of each cone-producing piñon tree in 2024 (Figure 7-12) and 2025 (Figure
7-13). Larger piñon trees produced more cones in 2024 (p < 0.05), but there was no correlation
between size and cone production in 2025. This monitoring project aims to document existing
conditions and inform decision-making to balance fire-risk reduction and conservation measures
for the pinyon jay.

The Soil, Foodstuffs, and Biota Program collects biological samples under an approved New
Mexico Game and Fish Scientific Collection Permit, a U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service Scientific
Collection Permit, and approved Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee protocols.
These procedures ensure that the collection, processing, and chemical analysis of samples; the
validation and verification of data; and the tabulation of analytical results are conducted in a
consistent manner from year to year. Locations and samples have unique identifiers to provide
chain-of-custody control from the time of collection through analysis and reporting.
The Health Physics Program calculates dose to nonhuman biota according to a written quality
control procedure, EPC-ES-TP-001, “Calculating Dose to Nonhuman Biota.” In addition,
procedures and protocols for biota dose assessment can be found in EPC-ES-TP-002, “Technical
Project Plan for Biota Dose Assessment.”
The Biological Resources Program collects field data and conducts compliance reviews
according to the following written technical procedures:
 • EPC-ES-AP-014, “Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee Operations”
 • EPC-ES-TP-214, “Project Reviews for Biological Resources”
 • EPC-ES-TP-204, “Noise Level Assessment and Measurement”
 • EPC-ES-TP-210, “Writing Biological Assessments”
 • EPC-ES-TP-203, “Threatened and Endangered Species Surveys”
 • EPC-ES-TP-205, “Avian Monitoring”
 • EPC-ES-TP-201, “Live Trapping of Small Mammals”

In addition to these procedures, some parts of our work require certain federal and state permits.
These permits are individual permits and not institutional. Personnel who work as wildlife
biologists must have the training and background to be able to obtain such permits. Surveys for
federally listed species follow specific protocols set forth by the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service,
and training to these protocols is a prerequisite to obtaining a permit. Permits include the
following:
 • Federal bird-banding permits issued by the U.S. Geological Survey’s bird-banding
 laboratory
 • Federal recovery permits to survey or handle federally listed species issued by the U.S.
 Fish and Wildlife Service. Training on species specific U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service
 protocols is required to apply for these federal permits.

 • State permits for scientific research issued by the New Mexico Department of Game and

Field Sampling Quality Assurance
Overall, the quality of field sampling is maintained through the rigorous use of carefully
documented procedures (listed in section Quality Assurance in this chapter) that govern all
aspects of the sample collection program.
We collect samples under full chain-of-custody procedures to minimize the chance of data
transcription errors. Once collected, we hand-deliver the samples to the Sample Management
Office, where staff ship the samples by express mail directly to an external analytical laboratory
under full chain-of-custody control. Sample Management Office personnel track all samples.
Upon receipt of data from the analytical laboratory, staff assess the completeness of the field
sample process and other variables. They create a quality assessment and provide it in the data
package. Field data completeness for sample collection in 2025 was 100 percent.
Water and equipment blanks are commonly used within analytical studies to determine if
contamination was inadvertently introduced into a sample set. In our investigation, we used
water and equipment blanks to determine if PFAS contamination had been introduced into field
samples through carryover from potentially contaminated equipment, experimental procedures,
or atmospheric conditions. We typically collect water blanks for PFAS detection during each
sampling event. In 2025, we collected three water blanks for PFAS, including background
samples. Water blanks were collected alongside large-mammal collections.
No PFAS-free water blank sample contained any detectable PFAS compounds.

Analytical Laboratory Quality Assessment

Total Analyte List
Bias arose when some inorganic element (e.g., magnesium, silver) results were obtained from
different analytical laboratories. For example, magnesium concentrations were notably lower
when analyzed at one analytical laboratory compared to the other; therefore, intercomparison of
inorganic elements via different analytical methods was avoided. Specifically, Australian
Laboratory Services (ALS) in Fort Collins, Colorado, was used for inorganic element analyses
until 2022; LANL then switched to GEL in Charleston, South Carolina.
Methods SW-846:6010 and SW-846:6020 are both Environmental Protection Agency–approved
inorganic determinative methods; however, method SW-846:6010 is performed via atomic
emission spectrometry and SW-846:6020 is performed via mass spectrometry (USEPA 2025). In
general, the SW-846:6020 method is more sensitive than SW-846:6010.
Although some consistencies exist in the analytical methods used at ALS and GEL, several
elements were analyzed differently. For example, at ALS, antimony and silver were analyzed
using SW-846:6020, then at GEL using SW-846:6010. Similarly, aluminum, beryllium,

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 7-37

chromium, cobalt, copper, iron, magnesium, and nickel, were analyzed using SW-846:6010 at
ALS, then by SW-846:6020 at GEL. Beginning in 2023, ALS and GEL used the same analytical
method for the remaining elements (arsenic, barium, cadmium, calcium, lead, manganese,
potassium, selenium, sodium, thallium, vanadium, and zinc).
When the analytical methods changed, some of values reported by GEL—both nondetects and
detects—were higher than the detected values that were reported by ALS. This discrepancy
introduced biases that affected the comparison of data between different analytical methods.
Therefore, intercomparison of inorganic elements using different analytical methods was avoided
in 2025 and onward. As such, the analytical method was accounted for when comparing
inorganic elements across locations and when calculating the regional statistical reference levels
(i.e., calculated on data from the same analytical method). Additionally, the bias prevented
incorporating inorganic elements into trend analyses; therefore, trend analyses were excluded on
datasets where the analytical method differed over the past 10 years.

Chapter 8 Public Dose and Risk Assessment

In this chapter, we assess the dose and risk from radiological and chemical releases to ensure that
the public is protected and to demonstrate compliance with federal regulations and U.S.
Department of Energy (DOE) orders. The data are analyzed using standard methods and models
to calculate potential effects of exposures for the public. The results are compared with
regulatory limits and international standards based on current knowledge of biological effects
caused by radiation.

Overview of Radiological Dose
Radiological dose is the primary measure of harm from radiation. We calculate doses using
standard DOE and U.S. Environmental Protection Agency methods (DOE 2022, DOE 2025, U.S.
Environmental Protection Agency 2020). In this chapter, we assess doses to the public. Doses to
plants and animals are assessed in Chapter 7.
DOE regulations limit the total annual dose to any member of the public from site operations to
100 millirem. The annual dose received by any member of the public from airborne emissions of
radionuclides is limited to 10 millirem by the National Emission Standards for Emissions of
Radionuclides Other Than Radon From Department of Energy Facilities, Title 40, Part 61,
Subpart H, of the Code of Federal Regulations. The annual dose from community drinking water
supplies is limited under the Safe Drinking Water Act to 4 millirem (National Primary Drinking
Water Regulations, Title 40, Part 141 of the Code of Federal Regulations).
To provide context for these limits, the dose from natural background and from medical and
dental procedures is about 800 millirem per year. Doses from site operations to members of the
public are typically less than 1 millirem per year. The Los Alamos County background dose is
discussed briefly in Dose from Naturally Occurring Radiation later in this chapter and in detail in
Gillis et al. (2014).

Exposure Pathways
Potential doses to the public from radionuclides associated with site operations are calculated by
evaluating all exposure pathways. DOE Order 458.1, Radiation Protection of the Public and the
Environment, lists the “likely exposure pathways” as follows:

 1. Direct external radiation from sources located on site is evaluated by the direct
 penetrating radiation monitoring network described in Chapter 4.
 2. External radiation from airborne radioactive material is evaluated using data from the
 ambient air and stack-emission-monitoring programs described in Chapter 4.
 3. External radiation from radioactive material deposited on surfaces off site is evaluated
 using the RESRAD program (https://resrad.evs.anl.gov/).
 4. Internal radiation from inhaled airborne radioactive material is evaluated using data from
 the ambient air and stack-emission-monitoring programs described in Chapter 4.
 5. Internal radiation from ingested radioactive material is evaluated using data from the
 Soil, Foodstuffs, and Biota program reported in Chapter 7 and Chapter 8.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 8-1

 6. Internal radiation from radioactive material ingested with aquatic food products,
 primarily in the Rio Grande, is sampled and reported in Chapter 7 every 3 years.
 7. External or internal radiation due to residual radioactive material on or in cleared
 property is monitored extensively in and near the historical Manhattan Project sites.

Los Alamos National Laboratory (LANL or Laboratory) is unique because Site Y of the
Manhattan Project was established near the center of the townsite and was driven by the urgency
of World War II (Groves 1962).

Direct Penetrating Radiation
We monitor direct penetrating radiation, such as gamma rays and neutrons, at 71 locations in and
around the Laboratory site (Chapter 4). Direct penetrating radiation from Laboratory sources
contributes to a measurable dose only within about 1 kilometer of the source. At distances of
more than 1 kilometer, dispersion, scattering, and absorption of photons and neutrons decrease
the dose to less than 0.1 millirem per year. Direct penetrating radiation that can be measured
above naturally occurring background radiation occurs on Laboratory property only within
Technical Areas 53 and 54, as reported in Chapter 4.

Air Pathways
At distances of more than 1 kilometer, exposure to radioactivity from site operations is mostly
the result of airborne radionuclides. We measure airborne radioactivity using the environmental
air-sampling network described in Chapter 4, Ambient Air Sampling for Radionuclides. We also
measure the emissions at the stacks as reported in Chapter 4, Exhaust-Stack Sampling for
Radionuclides. We use a standard computer modeling program called the Clean Air Act
Assessment Package 1988, PC Version 4.1 (CAP88) (https://www.epa.gov/radiation/cap88-pc;
U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 2013, 2020) to calculate the airborne radioactivity levels
and the resulting doses to the public. CAP88 calculates the internal dose from inhalation of
materials such as plutonium, as well as the external dose from airborne gamma-ray emitters such
as carbon-11 (McNaughton et al. 2017a,b).

Ingestion
Exposure through ingestion occurs when people consume liquids and food that contain
radionuclides. The ingestion pathway includes drinking local water or beverages prepared with
local water, eating locally grown food, and eating meat from either domesticated or hunted
animals that eat local vegetation or drink local water that contains radionuclides. Radioactivity
measurements of groundwater are reported in Chapter 5; measurements of surface water and
sediment are reported in Chapter 6; and measurements of soil, plants, and animals are reported in
Chapter 7. Foodstuffs are formally sampled once every 3 years, and when they are sampled, the
results are reported in this chapter.

Dose from Naturally Occurring Radiation
In Los Alamos County, naturally occurring sources of radioactivity include
 • cosmic rays;
 • direct penetrating radiation from the Earth due to radioactive elements in minerals, rocks,
 and soils;

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 8-2

 • radon gas; and
 • radioactive elements that occur naturally inside the human body, such as potassium-40.

Annual doses from cosmic radiation range from 50 millirem per year at lower elevations near the
Rio Grande to about 90 millirem per year in the higher elevations in mountains west of Los
Alamos (Bouville and Lowder 1988, Gillis et al. 2014). Annual background doses from external,
direct penetrating radiation from the Earth (from sources such as naturally occurring uranium
and thorium and their decay products) range from about 50 millirem to 150 millirem (DOE
2012).
The inhalation of radon gas and its decay products constitutes a large proportion of the annual
dose for members of the public. Nationwide, the average annual dose from radon is 200 to 300
millirem (National Council on Radiation Protection 1987). In Los Alamos County, the average
residential radon concentration results in an annual dose of about 300 millirem (Whicker and
McNaughton 2009). An additional 30 millirem per year results from naturally occurring
radioactive materials in the body, such as potassium-40, which is present in all food and living
cells.

Dose from Manufactured Products
Manufactured products that contain or use radiation also contribute to the total average annual
background dose (Gillis et al. 2014). Members of the US population receive an average annual
dose of 300 millirem from medical and dental uses of radiation (National Council on Radiation
Protection 2009). Another 10 millirem per year comes from building materials such as stone or
adobe walls.

Average Annual Background Dose
In total, the average annual dose from sources other than site operations is about 800 millirem for
a typical Los Alamos County resident. Figure 8-1 compares the average radiation background in
Los Alamos County with the average background dose in the United States. Generally, any dose
from site operations of less than 0.1 millirem per year cannot be distinguished from the dose
generated by background levels of radiation.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 8-3

Figure 8-1. The average Los Alamos County radiation background dose compared with average U.S.
 radiation background dose (Gillis et al. 2014). Note: K-40 = Potassium-40.

Individual Pathway Dose Calculations
Dose from Direct Penetrating Radiation
Results from the direct penetrating radiation monitoring network are described in Chapter 4 and
discussed in section Maximally Exposed Individual Onsite Dose later in this chapter.

Dose from Air Pathways
The CAP88 model is used to calculate inhalation and external radiation doses considering
meteorological data—such as humidity, temperatures, and wind direction and speed—along with
the monitoring data from stack emissions and the environmental air-monitoring network. These
calculations include ingestion doses and are described in detail in the Annual Radionuclide Air
Emissions Report (Fuehne, Lattin, and Didla 2026). The reported maximum potential dose to a
member of the public in 2025 was 0.65 millirem per year and is discussed further in section
Maximally Exposed Individual Offsite Dose later in this chapter.

Dose from Foodstuffs
Monitoring Network
The Soil, Foodstuffs, and Biota program monitors constituents in a wide variety of foodstuffs to
determine if Laboratory operations are affecting human health via the food chain. We collect
foodstuffs samples once every 3 years and most recently in 2025. We define foodstuffs as all
types of material that people might consume, including cultivated or native fruits and vegetables
and animal products such as eggs, milk, honey, meat from domestic or wild animals, and fish.
We use the word “crops” to refer to cultivated or native vegetative material.
In general, we collect foodstuffs from sites on the Laboratory, from communities surrounding the
Laboratory (perimeter locations), from areas downstream of the Laboratory that are irrigated
with Rio Grande water, and from background locations that are more than 15 kilometers
(9.3 miles) from the Laboratory and represent worldwide fallout or natural levels.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 8-4

In 2025, we collected approximately 80 crop samples, which included 28 different commodities:

  apples (both ordinary and crab varieties)  onions
  apricots  peaches
  basil  pears (both Asian and quince varieties)
  beans  plums
  cherries (ordinary, sour, and Nanking varieties)  potatoes
  cherry plums  purslane
  choke cherries  squash
  corn  Swiss chard
  cucumbers (both ordinary and lemon varieties)  tomatoes
  green chile  watermelons
  lambs quarters  zucchini
  Navajo spinach

We collected samples from the following sources (refer to Figure 8-2):
 • the Laboratory
 • gardens and farms located in Los Alamos townsite, White Rock/Pajarito Acres, Pueblo de
 San Ildefonso (perimeter locations), Pueblo de Cochiti and Peña Blanca (downstream of
 LANL)
 • regional background locations

As discussed in more detail in the following subsections, we collected chicken eggs from various
background locations, Cochiti Pueblo (downstream of LANL), and from perimeter locations
such as Los Alamos, White Rock, and Pueblo de San Ildefonso. We also collected milk, honey,
and tea from select locations. Additionally, we collect deer and elk samples on an annual basis,
primarily as roadkill or hunter donations. Results from deer and elk samples are reported in
Chapter 7. DOE Standard 1196 (DOE 2022) is used to calculate the dose from eating locally
grown food.

Methods and Analyses
We collected crop samples and rinsed them thoroughly with municipal tap water. We placed
crop samples into zippered plastic bags and eggs, milk, honey, and brewed-tea samples into
amber-colored glass sample bottles and polyethylene sample bottles. We labeled samples, sealed
them with chain-of-custody tape, placed them on ice, and submitted them to the Laboratory’s
Sample Management Office. All samples were shipped under full chain of custody to GEL
Laboratories, Charleston, South Carolina; Cape Fear Laboratory, Wilmington, North Carolina; or
Eurofins Environmental Testing, Sacramento, California, for analyses. At GEL Laboratories,
samples were analyzed for radionuclides (americium-241, cesium-137, plutonium-238,
plutonium-239/240, strontium-90, tritium, uranium-234, uranium-235/236, and uranium-238),
inorganic elements (aluminum, antimony, arsenic, barium, beryllium, cadmium, calcium,
chromium, cobalt, copper, iron, lead, magnesium, manganese, mercury, nickel, potassium,
selenium, silver, sodium, thallium, vanadium, and zinc). Crop samples were analyzed for per-
and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) at GEL Laboratories, and eggs, milk, honey, and cota tea
samples were analyzed for PFAS at Eurofins Environmental Testing. Milk and eggs were also
analyzed for polychlorinated biphenyl (PCB) congeners at Cape Fear Laboratory.
We compared sample results for radionuclides, inorganic elements, PCBs, or PFAS compounds
in foodstuffs with the regional statistical reference level for that constituent. The regional
statistical reference level is the level below which 99 percent of the results fell for samples
collected at regional background locations during the past 10 years (calculated from the mean
plus three standard deviations). For crops, the regional statistical reference level for a constituent
is calculated using all crops combined, not on a crop-specific basis. Total PCB concentrations in
milk and eggs were also compared with U.S. Food and Drug Administration’s Tolerances for
Polychlorinated Biphenyls (PCBs), Title 21 Part 190 Section 109.30 of the Code of Federal
Regulations (U.S. Food and Drug Administration 2023).

Results for Foodstuffs
Most radionuclide activities in crops were either below the minimum detectable activity
concentration or below the regional statistical reference level (Table S8-1). Americium-241,
plutonium-238, plutonium-239/240, and uranium-235/236 were not detected in any crop samples
(Table S8-1). Cesium-137, tritium, uranium-234, and uranium-238 were observed in at least one
crop sample, but all activities were below the regional statistical reference levels. Strontium-90
was the only radionuclide that was detected in lambs quarters at 4.80 picocuries per gram, which
exceeded the regional statistical reference level of 3.34 picocuries per gram; this sample was
collected in Los Alamos Canyon near the weir on LANL property (Table S8-1). Because the
location is within LANL property and not accessible to the public, it does not contribute to
public dose.
We collected chicken eggs from Los Alamos, White Rock, Cochiti Pueblo, Pueblo de San
Ildefonso, and background locations. We collected goat milk from Los Alamos, White Rock, and
background locations. No radionuclides were detected in any of the chicken egg or goat milk
samples (Table S8-2).
No radionuclides were detected in the honey sample collected at the Dual-Axis Radiographic
Hydrodynamic Test Facility at LANL; refer to LANL 2026 for results.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 8-7

We collected cota, a native plant that can be used to make tea, from LANL, Los Alamos, White
Rock, Cochiti Pueblo, Pueblo de San Ildefonso, and background locations. We brewed cota tea
in the laboratory and used PFAS-free water that was provided to us by Eurofins. No
radionuclides were detected in any of the tea samples (Table S8-3).
Overall, the data support the conclusion that the ingestion dose caused by LANL operations is
much less than 0.1 millirem per year. Radionuclide concentrations in publicly available food are
consistent with global fallout or naturally occurring material, and any contributions from the site
are too small to measure and consistent with zero.

Dose from Water
We report measurements from water in Chapter 5 and Chapter 6. Local drinking water contains
no measurable radioactivity from current or historical site operations. For further information
regarding Los Alamos County drinking water, refer to the Los Alamos Department of Public
Utilities 2025 Annual Drinking Water Quality Report (Los Alamos County 2026). The dose
pathway from surface water to humans is through foodstuffs, discussed previously in Dose from
Foodstuffs.

Dose from Soil
Radioactive materials in soil can contribute to dose by any of the exposure pathways discussed
previously. Potential doses are calculated using the RESRAD family of codes, which analyze
potential human and biota radiation exposures from residual radioactive materials in the
environment (https://resrad.evs.anl.gov/).
In 2025, soil samples were collected by the Soil, Foodstuffs, and Biota Program and are reported
in Chapter 7. The results are similar to previous years and support the conclusion that the
ingestion dose is much less than 0.1 millirem per year.

All-Pathway Radiological Dose Calculations
As required by DOE Order 458.1 Chg 5, Radiation Protection of the Public and the Environment,
we calculated doses from the site to the total human population that reside within 80 kilometers
(50 miles) of the site and to the hypothetical “maximally exposed individual.” The maximally
exposed individual represents a person who does not work at the site and who, because of their
location and activities, has the potential to receive the largest radiation dose (DOE 2025).

Collective Dose to the Population within 80 Kilometers
The collective population dose from site operations is the sum of the doses for each member of
the public within an 80-kilometer radius of the site (DOE 2025). Outside of Los Alamos County,
the doses are too small to measure directly, so the collective dose was calculated using CAP88.
In 2025, the collective population dose was 0.34 person-rem (Fuehne, Lattin, and Didla 2026).
Collective population doses for recent years are provided in Figure 8-3. The trend line shows a
general decrease, which is the result of improved engineering controls at the Los Alamos
Neutron Science Center and tritium facilities. The increase during 2025 was the result of
increased operations at many Laboratory facilities and cannot be attributed to a single cause. As
summarized in Table 8-1, the collective dose of 0.34 person-rem from the Laboratory is much

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 8-8

smaller than the background of 285,000 person-rem and so does not add significantly to the
public dose.

Figure 8-3. Annual collective dose (person-rem) to the population within 80 kilometers of the Laboratory.

Dose to the Maximally Exposed Individual
To identify the location of and total dose to the hypothetical maximally exposed individual, we
consider all exposure pathways that could cause a dose and all publicly accessible locations, both
within the site boundaries (on site) and outside the boundaries (off site).

Maximally Exposed Individual Offsite Dose
In 2025, the offsite location of the hypothetical maximally exposed individual was on DP Road
close to environmental air-monitoring station #328, which is near the east end of the business
section of DP Road (Figure 4-1). The total offsite dose for the maximally exposed individual
during 2025 was 0.65 millirem (Fuehne, Lattin, and Didla 2026).
Contributions to this annual dose were from measurements at the environmental air-monitoring
station #328 (0.487 millirem), the potential dose contribution from unmonitored stacks (0.156
millirem), and other stacks (<0.01 mrem). As described in the 2025 LANL Radionuclide Air
Emissions Report (Fuehne, Lattin, and Didla 2026), these measurements are based on
conservative assumptions.
The annual maximally exposed individual doses are provided in Figure 8-4. The general
downward trend is the result of improved engineering controls. As described in previous annual
site environmental reports, the 6.46 millirem dose in 2005 resulted from a leak at Technical Area
53, and the 3.53 millirem dose in 2011 was from the remediation of Material Disposal Area B.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 8-9

Figure 8-4. Annual maximally exposed individual dose.

Maximally Exposed Individual Onsite Dose
The only publicly accessible onsite location with a measurable dose from site operations is on
East Jemez Road near Technical Area 53 (McNaughton et al. 2013). The dose from stack
emissions at this location was calculated assuming that a member of the public was on East
Jemez Road in the canyon with a plume of airborne emissions that originated from the mesa-top
facilities. Calculations using CAP88 and the methods of McNaughton et al. (2017a, 2017b)
found that the 2025 maximally exposed individual onsite dose from stack emissions was less
than 0.1 mrem. As reported in Chapter 4, at this location in 2025, the neutron dose was 0.23
millirem, and the gamma dose was 0.012 millirem, for a total dose of less than 0.4 millirem. This
dose would be received by an individual who stayed at this location 24 hours per day for 365
days per year. However, members of the public—such as joggers, cyclists, or bus drivers—spend
no more than 1 hour per week at this location, an occupancy factor of approximately 1/167
(National Council on Radiation Protection 2005). Therefore, after applying the occupancy factor,
the dose for a maximally exposed individual on site is less than 0.01 millirem.
As reported in Chapter 4, neutron dose was measured in Cañada del Buey (north of Technical
Area 54, Area G) and near the Pueblo de San Ildefonso boundary. Transuranic waste at Area G
emits neutrons while awaiting shipment to the Waste Isolation Pilot Plant in Carlsbad, New
Mexico. After subtracting background, the measured neutron dose in Cañada del Buey in 2025
was 4 millirem. After applying the standard occupancy factor of 1/20 for occasional occupancy
(National Council on Radiation Protection 2005), the individual neutron dose in 2025 was 0.2
millirem. Using the dose conversion factors from DOE Standard 1196 (DOE 2022) and assuming
1/20 occupancy, the inhalation dose in Cañada del Buey from radioactive material at Area G was
less than 0.01 millirem. Thus in 2025, the total dose in Cañada del Buey from site operations at
Area G was approximately 0.2 millirem.

Los Alamos National Laboratory 2025 Annual Site Environmental Report Page 8-10

Maximally Exposed Individual Summary
At the location for the maximally exposed individual at DP Road, the direct penetrating radiation
and ingestion doses are consistent with zero, so the maximum all-pathway dose for 2025 was
0.65 millirem (Fuehne, Lattin, and Didla 2026).
The dose of 0.65 millirem in 2025 is far below the 10 millirem annual air-pathway limit in the
National Emission Standards for Emissions of Radionuclides Other Than Radon From
Department of Energy Facilities (Title 40, Part 61, Subpart H of the Code of Federal
Regulations), and the 100 millirem all-pathway DOE limit (DOE 2025). The dose for the
maximally exposed individual is less than 0.1 percent of the average US background radiation
dose presented in Figure 8-1.
